<!-- defn-1: body_md -->
\label{def:regular}
A decreasing function $\psi$ is called \emph{lower quasimultiplicative}
if there exist $0<\tau<\infty$ and $R_0>1$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:1.3}
 R^{-\tau}\psi(T)\leqslant\psi(RT)
\end{equation}
for every sufficiently large $T$ and every $R\geqslant R_0$.
<!-- end defn-1 -->
<!-- thm-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:main}
Let $A\in\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}$ and let $\psi$ be continuous, strictly decreasing,
lower quasimultiplicative, and tend to zero.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item The limits
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pressure-minus}
 P_-(A,\psi):=
 \lim_{L\to\infty}\inf_{c>0}\mathcal P_{A,\psi}(L,c)
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation*}\label{eq:pressure-plus}
 P_+(A,\psi):=
 \lim_{L\to\infty}\sup_{c>0}\mathcal P_{A,\psi}(L,c)
\end{equation*}
exist. One has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:1.15}
 \dim_H{\mathrm{Sing}}_A(\psi)
 =\inf_{c>0}\dim_HD_A(c\psi)
 =P_-(A,\psi)
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:1.16}
 \dim_H U_A(\psi)
 =\sup_{c>0}\dim_HD_A(c\psi)
 =P_+(A,\psi).
\end{equation}
\item The quantity $\dim_HD_A(c\psi)$ is independent of $c>0$
if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:1.17}
 P_-(A,\psi)=P_+(A,\psi).
\end{equation}
Under this $c$-invariance condition,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:1.18}
 \begin{split}
 \dim_HD_A(\psi)
 &=\dim_H{\mathrm{Sing}}_A(\psi)=\dim_H U_A(\psi)\\
 &=P_-(A,\psi)=P_+(A,\psi)
 =\lim_{L\to\infty}\mathcal P_{A,\psi}(L,1).
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end thm-1 -->
<!-- rem-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:generic-c-invariance}
The value $\dim_HD_A(c\psi)$ is independent of $c$ (in which case Theorem~\ref{thm:main} gives the exact expression for $\dim_HD_A(\psi)$) generically along power families, in the following precise sense:
for every fixed $A$ and every lower quasimultiplicative $\psi$, the dimension $\dim_HD_A(c\psi^s)$ is independent of $c>0$ for all but countably many $s>0$. Indeed, if $0<s<t$, then
$c_2\psi(T)^t\leqslant c_1\psi(T)^s$ eventually for any $c_1,c_2>0$.
Theorem~\ref{thm:main} therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-pressure-ordering}
 P_+(A,\psi^t)\leqslant P_-(A,\psi^s).
\end{equation}
The nonempty intervals
$(P_-(A,\psi^s),P_+(A,\psi^s))$ are consequently pairwise disjoint,
so there are only countably many of them. Outside this exceptional
set, \eqref{eq:1.18} gives the exact dimension for every $c>0$.
The exceptional set here may depend on $A$ and $\psi$.
In Section~\ref{subsec:near-powers}, for functions similar to power functions, we give an explicit finite set containing all possible exceptional powers, which is {independent} of $A$.
<!-- end rem-1 -->
<!-- thm-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:near-powers}
Let $A\in\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}$ and $\tau\in(0,\infty)\setminus\mathcal E_{m,n}$.
For every $c>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-rank-dimension}
 \dim_HD_A(c\psi_\tau)
 =\liminf_{T\to\infty}
 \frac{\displaystyle\int_1^T r_{A,\tau}(S)\,\frac{dS}{S}
       -\log\mathcal N_A(T^{-\tau},T)}{(1+\tau)\log T}.
\end{equation}
In particular, this dimension is independent of $c$. Its common value
is continuous as a function of $\tau$ on each component of
$(0,\infty)\setminus\mathcal E_{m,n}$.

Moreover, if $\psi$ is continuous, strictly decreasing to zero, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:limiting-power-order}
 \lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{\log\psi(T)^{-1}}{\log T}=\tau,
\end{equation}
then, for every $c>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:near-power-dimension}
 \dim_HD_A(c\psi)=\dim_HD_A(\psi_\tau),
\end{equation}
so the same right-hand side of \eqref{eq:power-rank-dimension} gives
the dimension for $c\psi$. No lower quasimultiplicativity is required in
this last assertion.
<!-- end thm-2 -->
<!-- rem-2: body_md -->
\label{rem:exceptional-powers} 
It is necessary to exclude the finite set of exceptional exponents from consideration in Theorem \ref{thm:near-powers}: for each of these exponents there is a matrix $A$ such that $\dim_HD_A(c\psi_\tau)$ is not $c$-invariant. Fix $\tau=b/a\in\mathcal E_{m,n}$, choose a badly approximable matrix $B\in\mathrm{Mat}_{a,b}$, and let $A\in\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}$ have $B$ as its upper-left block and all other
entries zero. Then
\[
 D_A(c\psi_\tau)
 =D_B(c\psi_\tau)\times\{\mathbf0\}
 \subseteq\mathbb T^a\times\{\mathbf0\}.
\]
Bad approximability is preserved by transposition. Hence
Propositions~\ref{prop:trivial-shifts}
and~\ref{prop:transference-full}, applied to $B$, give constants
$0<c_0<c_1$ such that
\[
 D_A(c\psi_\tau)=
 \begin{cases}
 \langle B\mathbb Z^b \rangle\times\{\mathbf0\},
       &0<c<c_0,\\
 \mathbb T^a\times\{\mathbf0\},
       &c>c_1.
 \end{cases}
\]
Here $\langle \cdot \rangle$ denotes reduction modulo $\mathbb Z^a$. Their dimensions are respectively $0$ and $a$. 

We do not claim, however, that each exponent in $\mathcal E_{m,n}$ is exceptional for every matrix $A$.
<!-- end rem-2 -->
<!-- prop-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:uniform-exponent-levels}
For every $A\in\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}$ and every $\tau>0$,
\begin{align}
 \dim_H\{\mathbf b\in\mathbb T^m:\widehat\omega(A,\mathbf b)\geqslant\tau\}
 &=\lim_{s\nearrow\tau}P_\pm(A,\psi_s) = \inf_{0<\sigma<\tau}\dim_HD_A(\psi_\sigma),
 \label{eq:exponent-superlevel-dimension}\\
 \dim_H\{\mathbf b\in\mathbb T^m:\widehat\omega(A,\mathbf b)>\tau\}
 &=\lim_{s\searrow\tau}P_\pm(A,\psi_s) = \sup_{\sigma>\tau}\dim_HD_A(\psi_\sigma).
 \label{eq:exponent-strict-superlevel-dimension}
\end{align}
All the involved limits exist; one can choose both $+$ and $-$ in the expression above. If the set $\{\mathbf b:\widehat\omega(A,\mathbf b)=\tau\}$ is nonempty,
then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:exact-exponent-dimension}
 \dim_H\{\mathbf b\in\mathbb T^m:\widehat\omega(A,\mathbf b)=\tau\}
 =\lim_{s\nearrow\tau}P_-(A,\psi_s).
\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-1 -->
<!-- cor-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:noncritical-exponent-levels}
If $\tau\notin\mathcal E_{m,n}$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:noncritical-exponent-dimension}
\begin{aligned}
 \dim_H\{\mathbf b\in\mathbb T^m:\widehat\omega(A,\mathbf b)\geqslant\tau\}
 &=\dim_H\{\mathbf b\in\mathbb T^m:\widehat\omega(A,\mathbf b)>\tau\}\\
 &=\dim_HD_A(\psi_\tau).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The same equality holds for the exact level
$\{\mathbf b:\widehat\omega(A,\mathbf b)=\tau\}$ whenever it is nonempty.
Thus these dimensions are given by \eqref{eq:power-rank-dimension}.
<!-- end cor-1 -->
<!-- lem-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:rescaling-comparison}
Suppose that $\psi$ is lower quasimultiplicative. For every $c>0$
there are constants $a_c,b_c>0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rescaling-comparison}
 a_c\psi(T)\leqslant\widetilde\psi_c(T)\leqslant b_c\psi(T)
\end{equation}
for all sufficiently large $T$. Consequently, the definitions in
\eqref{eq:classical-endpoints} and \eqref{eq:1.14} agree.
<!-- end lem-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Inequalities \eqref{eq:rescaling-comparison} follow from
\eqref{eq:lower-qm-comparison}, applied with
$R=\max\{c,c^{-1}\}$ when $c\ne1$; the case $c=1$ is immediate.
They give one inclusion in each comparison of the intersections
and unions in \eqref{eq:classical-endpoints} and \eqref{eq:1.14}.
For the reverse inclusions, observe that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:elementary-rescaling-comparison}
 \widetilde\psi_c(T)\leqslant c\psi(T)\quad(0<c\leqslant1),
 \qquad
 \widetilde\psi_c(T)\geqslant c\psi(T)\quad(c\geqslant1).
\end{equation}
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- thm-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:zero-full}
Let $A\in\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}$ and let $\psi$ be continuous, strictly decreasing,
and tend to zero.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item\label{enu:zero-full-amplification}
Let $f$ be a dimension function. There exists $h_0\in[0,+\infty]$
such that, for $b>0$,
\begin{equation}
\mathcal H^f\bigl(D_A(\widetilde\psi_b)\bigr) = \begin{cases}
 \mathcal H^f(H_A),& b>h_0,\\
 0,& 0<b<h_0.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
In the first case, $D_A(\widetilde\psi_b)$ actually has locally full
$\mathcal H^f$-measure in $H_A$.
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Haar-amplification}
 \mathcal L_A\bigl(D_A(\widetilde\psi_a)\bigr)>0
 \ \Rightarrow\
 \mathcal L_A\bigl(D_A(\widetilde\psi_b)\bigr)=1 \quad \text{for any} \ b > a.
\end{equation}
\item\label{enu:zero-full-exact}
Let $a>0$. If
\[
 \mathcal L_A\bigl(D_A(\widetilde\psi_a)\bigr)=1,
\]
then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:exact-amplification}
 D_A(\widetilde\psi_{2a})=H_A.
\end{equation}
\item\label{enu:zero-full-subgroups}
The sets ${\mathrm{Sing}}_A(\psi)$ and $ U_A(\psi)$ are Borel
subgroups of $H_A$. For every dimension function $f$, each has
either zero or locally full $\mathcal H^f$-measure in $H_A$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end thm-3 -->
<!-- prop-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:endpoint-representations}
Under the assumptions of Theorem \ref{thm:main}, the following hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item\label{enu:upper-intersection}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:1.28}
  U_A(\psi)
 =\bigcap_{\psi=o(\varphi)}D_A(\varphi).
\end{equation}
The intersection in \eqref{eq:1.28} is unchanged if it is restricted to
lower quasimultiplicative functions.

\item\label{enu:lower-union}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sing-union}
 {\mathrm{Sing}}_A(\psi)
 =\bigcup_{\vartheta=o(\psi)}D_A(\vartheta).
\end{equation}
The union in \eqref{eq:sing-union} is unchanged if it is restricted to
lower quasimultiplicative functions.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end prop-2 -->
<!-- prop-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:dimension-continuity}
Under the assumptions of Theorem \ref{thm:main},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dimension-continuity}
\begin{split}
 \dim_H{\mathrm{Sing}}_A(\psi)
 &=\sup_{\vartheta=o(\psi)}\dim_HD_A(\vartheta)
 \leqslant \dim_HD_A(\psi)\\
 &\leqslant \inf_{\psi=o(\varphi)}\dim_HD_A(\varphi)
 =\dim_H U_A(\psi).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
The supremum and the infimum in \eqref{eq:dimension-continuity} are
unchanged if they are restricted to lower quasimultiplicative functions.
<!-- end prop-3 -->
<!-- head-1: body_md -->
For nonnegative quantities $X$ and $Y$, we write $X\ll Y$ if
$X\leqslant CY$ for some constant $C>0$, write $X\gg Y$ if $Y\ll X$,
and write $X\asymp Y$ if both $X\ll Y$ and $Y\ll X$.
For an arbitrary real quantity $X$ and a positive quantity $Y$, we
write $X=O(Y)$ if $|X|\ll Y$, and write $X=o(Y)$ if $X/Y\to0$ in the
limit under consideration.
Since the dimensions $m,n$, the matrix $A$, and the function $\psi$
are fixed throughout, dependence on them is suppressed in
$\ll,\gg,\asymp$, $O(\cdot)$, and $o(\cdot)$.
Dependence on any additional parameters is recorded by subscripts.
<!-- end head-1 -->
<!-- thm-4: body_md -->
\label{cor:kim-liao}
Let $\alpha \in \mathbb R$ be irrational and $c > 0$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item If $0<\tau<1/\omega(\alpha)$, then
${D_{\alpha,\mathbb N}}(c\psi_\tau)=\mathbb T$.
\item If $\tau>\omega(\alpha)$, then
\[
 {D_{\alpha,\mathbb N}}(c\psi_\tau)=\{q\alpha:q \in \mathbb N \}.
\]
\item If $1/\omega(\alpha)<\tau<1$, fix $a > 0$ and let $\{n_i\}$ be the increasing
enumeration of the convergent denominators satisfying
\begin{equation*}\label{eq:5.91}
 n_i|n_i\alpha|_{\mathbb Z}^{\tau}<a.
\end{equation*}
Then
\begin{equation*}\label{eq:5.92}
 \dim_H{D_{\alpha,\mathbb N}}(c\psi_\tau)
 =
 \liminf_{i\to\infty}
 \frac{
 \displaystyle
 \log\left(
 n_i^{1+1/\tau}
 \prod_{j=1}^{i-1}
 n_j^{1/\tau}|n_j\alpha|_{\mathbb Z}
 \right)}
 {\displaystyle
 \log\left(n_i|n_i\alpha|_{\mathbb Z}^{-1}\right)}.
\end{equation*}
\item If $1<\tau<\omega(\alpha)$, fix $b > 0$ and let $\{n_i\}$ be the increasing
enumeration of the convergent denominators satisfying
\begin{equation*}\label{eq:5.93}
 n_i^\tau|n_i\alpha|_{\mathbb Z}<b.
\end{equation*}
Then
\begin{equation*}\label{eq:5.94}
 \dim_H{D_{\alpha,\mathbb N}}(c\psi_\tau)
 =
 \liminf_{i\to\infty}
 \frac{
 \displaystyle
 -\log\left(
 \prod_{j=1}^{i-1}
 n_j|n_j\alpha|_{\mathbb Z}^{1/\tau}
 \right)}
 {\displaystyle
 \log\left(n_i|n_i\alpha|_{\mathbb Z}^{-1}\right)}.
\end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end thm-4 -->
<!-- thm-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:one-dimensional}
Suppose that $\psi$ is {continuous, strictly decreasing to zero,
and lower quasimultiplicative}.  Then the following statements hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item {Suppose that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:one-dimensional-slow-hypothesis}
 \limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{\log\psi(T)^{-1}}{\log T}<1,
\end{equation}
}and let $\{k_i\}$ be the increasing
enumeration of all sufficiently large indices satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{eq:5.59}
 \psi\bigl(|q_{k_i}\alpha|_{\mathbb Z}^{-1}\bigr)<q_{k_i}^{-1}.
\end{equation}
If this sequence is finite, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:5.60}
 \dim_HD_\alpha(\psi)=1.
\end{equation}
If it is infinite, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:5.61}
 \dim_HD_\alpha(\psi)
 =
 \liminf_{i\to\infty}
 \frac{
 \displaystyle
 \log\left(
 q_{k_i}\prod_{j=1}^{i-1}
 q_{k_j}\psi(Y_{k_j})
 \right)}
 {\log\psi(Y_{k_i})^{-1}}.
\end{equation}

\item {Suppose that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:one-dimensional-fast-hypothesis}
 \liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac{\log\psi(T)^{-1}}{\log T}>1,
\end{equation}
}and let $\{k_i\}$ be the increasing
enumeration of all sufficiently large indices satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{eq:5.62}
 \psi(q_{k_i})>|q_{k_i}\alpha|_{\mathbb Z}.
\end{equation}
If this sequence is finite, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:5.63}
 \dim_HD_\alpha(\psi)=0.
\end{equation}
If it is infinite, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:5.64}
 \dim_HD_\alpha(\psi)
 =
 \liminf_{i\to\infty}
 \frac{
 \displaystyle
 \log\left(
 \prod_{j=1}^{i-1}
 \frac{\psi(Y_{k_j})}{|q_{k_j}\alpha|_{\mathbb Z}}
 \right)}
 {\log\psi(Y_{k_i})^{-1}}.
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
In either regime, for every fixed $c>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:5.65}
 \dim_HD_\alpha(c\psi)
 =
 \dim_HD_\alpha(\widetilde\psi_c)
 =
 \dim_HD_\alpha(\psi).
\end{equation}
<!-- end thm-5 -->
<!-- rem-3: body_md -->
\label{rem:for-KL}
Due to the last assertion of Remark \ref{rem:lower-Q}, the above theorem also holds if one replaces the set $D_\alpha(\psi)$ by $D_{\alpha, \mathbb N}(\psi)$ defined in \eqref{eq:D_alpha_N}.
Due to the $c$-invariance \eqref{eq:5.65} and trivial inclusion property 
$$
D_{\alpha, \mathbb N}(\widetilde{\psi}_c) \subseteq D_{\alpha, \mathbb N}(\psi)
$$
which holds for $c < 1$, one can also use strict inequalities in the definition \eqref{eq:D_alpha_N}.
<!-- end rem-3 -->
<!-- prop-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:aggarwal-special}
For every $A\in\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}$,
\begin{equation}
 \dim_H{\mathrm{Sing}}_A(\psi_{\frac nm})
 \leqslant
 m{\frac{d-1}{d}}\,\underline{\operatorname{Emass}}(A).
\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-4 -->
<!-- prop-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:aggarwal-sharpness}
For every $0\leqslant\eta\leqslant1$, there exists $A\in\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}$ such that
\[
 \underline{\operatorname{Emass}}(A)=\eta
 \qquad \text{and} \qquad
 \dim_H{\mathrm{Sing}}_A(\psi_{n/m})=\eta\,\frac{m(d-1)}d.
\]
<!-- end prop-5 -->
<!-- ex-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:aggarwal-sharp-example}
Let $\alpha=[0;2,3,4,\ldots]$. Then
\[
 \underline{\operatorname{Emass}}(\alpha)=1,
 \qquad
 \dim_H{\mathrm{Sing}}_\alpha(\psi_1)
 =\dim_HD_\alpha(c\psi_1)=\frac12
 \quad\text{for every }c>0.
\]
<!-- end ex-1 -->
<!-- prop-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:aggarwal-general}
For every $A\in\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}$ and every $\tau>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:5.31}
 \dim_H{\mathrm{Sing}}_A(\psi_\tau)
 \leqslant
 \lim_{\delta\searrow0}\liminf_{T\to\infty}
 \frac{\displaystyle\int_1^T r_{A,\tau}(S;\delta)\,\frac{dS}{S}
       -\log\mathcal N_A(T^{-\tau},T)}{(1+\tau)\log T}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-6 -->
<!-- prop-7: body_md -->
\label{prop:transference-null}
Let $\psi$ and $\varphi$ be dual. If $A^\top$ is
$\varphi$-approximable, then, for every $0<\varepsilon<1/(2d)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:transference-null}
 \mathcal L^m\bigl(D_A(\widetilde\psi_\varepsilon)\bigr)=0.
\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-7 -->
<!-- prop-8: body_md -->
\label{prop:transference-full}
Suppose that $\psi$ and $\varphi$ are dual and that $A^\top$ is not
$\varphi$-approximable. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:transference-full}
 D_A(\widetilde\psi_{\kappa_d})=\mathbb T^m.
\end{equation}
Moreover, under the same assumption,
\[
 D_{A,\mathbb N^n}(\widetilde\psi_{3\kappa_d})=\mathbb T^m,
\]
where $D_{A,\mathcal Q}(\psi)$ is defined in
Remark~\ref{rem:lower-Q}.
<!-- end prop-8 -->
<!-- prop-9: body_md -->
\label{prop:trivial-shifts}
If $A$ is not $\varphi$-approximable, then, for every $0<c<1/2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:5.51}
 D_A(\widetilde\varphi_c)=\Gamma_A.
\end{equation}
Moreover, under the same assumption, for every
$\mathcal Q\subseteq\mathbb Z^n$ one has
\[
 D_{A,\mathcal Q}(\widetilde\varphi_c)
 =A\mathcal Q\bmod\mathbb Z^m.
\]
<!-- end prop-9 -->
<!-- prop-10: body_md -->
\label{prop:upper}
For every sequence $\{T_j\}_{j\geqslant1}$ as above,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:2.4}
{\dim_H}\bigl(D_A(\psi)\bigr)
{\leqslant}
\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}
{\frac{\displaystyle
(\ell-1)\log C_d+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell-1}\log
\frac{\mathcal N_A(\psi(T_j),T_{j+1})}
{\mathcal N_A(\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1})}}
{-\log\psi(T_\ell)}},
\end{equation}
where $C_d\geqslant1$ depends only on $d$.
<!-- end prop-10 -->
<!-- prop-11: body_md -->
\label{prop:lower}
Suppose that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:2.5}
 \psi(T_j)\leqslant\rho\psi(T_{j+1}),\qquad j\geqslant1,
\end{equation}
for some $\rho>1$.  {Then}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:2.6}
\begin{split}
{\dim_H}\bigl(D_A(\rho\psi)\bigr)
{\geqslant}{}&
\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}{\frac{1}{-\log\psi(T_\ell)}}\\
&{\quad\times\sum_{j=1}^{\ell-1}\log}
\max\Biggl\{1,c_d
\frac{\mathcal N_A(\psi(T_j)-\psi(T_{j+1}),
 T_{j+1}-T_j)}
{\mathcal N_A(\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1})}
\Biggr\},
\end{split}
\end{equation}
where $c_d>0$ is a constant depending only on $d$.

If, in addition, there exists $\eta>1$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:2.7}
 T_{j+1}\geqslant\eta T_j\ \ \ \text{and} \ \ 
 \psi(T_j)\geqslant\eta\psi(T_{j+1})
 \ \ \text{for any} \ \ j\geqslant1,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:2.8}
\begin{split}
{\dim_H}\bigl(D_A(\rho\psi)\bigr)
{\geqslant}{}&
\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}{\frac{1}{-\log\psi(T_\ell)}}\\
&{\quad\times\sum_{j=1}^{\ell-1}\log}
\max\Biggl\{1,c_d\left(\frac{\eta-1}{\eta}\right)^d
\frac{\mathcal N_A(\psi(T_j),T_{j+1})}
{\mathcal N_A(\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1})}
\Biggr\}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-11 -->
<!-- rem-4: body_md -->
\label{rem:lower-Q}
For a nonempty set $\mathcal Q\subseteq\mathbb Z^n$, let
$D_{A,\mathcal Q}(\psi)$ be defined by \eqref{eq:1.1}, with the
additional requirement $\mathbf q\in\mathcal Q$ in \eqref{eq:1.2}. Suppose
that $\mathcal Q$ satisfies
\begin{equation*}\tag{S}\label{eq:central-extension}
 \forall \ \text{$\mathbf q\in\mathcal Q$ and $\mathbf x\in\mathbb Z^n$, at least one of }
 \mathbf q+\mathbf x\text{ and }\mathbf q-\mathbf x\text{ belongs to }\mathcal Q.
\end{equation*}
Then Proposition \ref{prop:lower} holds with
$D_A(\rho\psi)$ replaced by
$D_{A,\mathcal Q}(\rho\psi)$, after possibly replacing $c_d$ by
another positive constant depending only on $d$.

As a consequence, under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:main}, put
\begin{equation*}\label{eq:restricted-classical-endpoints}
 {\mathrm{Sing}}_{A,\mathcal Q}(\psi):=
 \bigcap_{c>0}D_{A,\mathcal Q}(c\psi),
 \qquad
  U_{A,\mathcal Q}(\psi):=
 \bigcup_{c>0}D_{A,\mathcal Q}(c\psi).
\end{equation*}
Then Theorem~\ref{thm:main} remains valid with
$D_A(\psi)$, ${\mathrm{Sing}}_A(\psi)$ and $ U_A(\psi)$
replaced by their $\mathcal Q$-restricted counterparts.
<!-- end rem-4 -->
<!-- lem-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:lattice-count}
Let $\Lambda$ be a full-rank lattice in $\mathbb R^d$.  Then, for every $r>0$,
\begin{equation*}\label{eq:3.2}
 \#\bigl(\Lambda\cap r\mathcal B\bigr)
 \asymp_d
 \prod_{i=1}^d\max\left\{\frac r{\lambda_i(\Lambda)},1\right\}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lem-2 -->
<!-- lem-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:profile-count}
One has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:3.3}
 N_A(R,Q)\asymp_d \mathcal N_A(R,Q)
\end{equation}
uniformly in $Q,R>0$.
<!-- end lem-3 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Note that by definition
$$
 N_A(R,Q)
 =
 \#\left(
 u_A\mathbb Z^d
 \cap [-R,R]^m\times[-Q,Q]^n
 \right).
$$
The diagonal map 
$$
a_{{t_{R,Q}}} = \begin{pmatrix}
\left( Q/R \right)^{\frac{n}{d}}I_m&0\\
 0& \left( Q/R \right)^{-\frac{m}{d}} I_n
 \end{pmatrix}
$$ takes the rectangle
\[
 [-R,R]^m\times[-Q,Q]^n
\]
to the cube of radius $Q^{n/d}R^{m/d}$.  Therefore
\[
 N_A(R,Q)
 =
 \#\left(
 a_{{t_{R,Q}}}u_A\mathbb Z^d
 \cap Q^{n/d}R^{m/d}\mathcal B
 \right),
\]
and  
Lemma \ref{lem:lattice-count} yields that
\begin{equation}
    N_A(R,Q) \asymp_d
 \prod_{i=1}^d\max\left\{\frac {Q^{n/d}R^{m/d}}{\lambda_i({t_{R,Q}})},1\right\} = \mathcal N_A(R, Q),
\end{equation}
proving \eqref{eq:3.3}.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lem-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:profile-properties}
The function $\mathcal N_A(R,Q)$ is nondecreasing in each argument. Moreover, for
every $R,Q,\beta>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:profile-scaling}
 \min\{1,\beta^d\}\mathcal N_A(R,Q)
 \leqslant \mathcal N_A(\beta R,\beta Q)
 \leqslant\max\{1,\beta^d\}\mathcal N_A(R,Q).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lem-4 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
It follows from \eqref{eq:2.1} that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rectangular-profile}
 \mathcal N_A(R,Q)
 =\prod_{i=1}^d\max\left\{
 \lambda_i\left([-R,R]^m\times[-Q,Q]^n,u_A\mathbb Z^d\right)^{-1},1
 \right\}.
\end{equation}
Monotonicity follows since
enlarging a convex body decreases each of its successive minima.
Multiplying both arguments by $\beta$ dilates the rectangle by $\beta$
and divides its minima by $\beta$. Thus \eqref{eq:profile-scaling} follows
from the inequalities
\[
 \min\{1,\beta\}\max\{x,1\}
 \leqslant\max\{\beta x,1\}
 \leqslant\max\{1,\beta\}\max\{x,1\},\qquad x>0.
\]
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lem-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:translated-count}
Let $(\mathbf x,\mathbf q_0)\in u_A\mathbb Z^d$, where $\mathbf x\in\mathbb R^m, \ \mathbf q_0 \in \mathbb Z^n$, and let $Q,R>0$.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lattice-translation-count}
 \#\left(u_A\mathbb Z^d\cap
 \bigl((\mathbf x+[-R,R]^m)\times(\mathbf q_0+[-Q,Q]^n)\bigr)\right)
 =N_A(R,Q).
\end{equation}
If $|\mathbf q_0|\leqslant Q$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:translated-count-window}
\begin{split}
 N_A(R,Q)
 &\leqslant\#\left(u_A\mathbb Z^d\cap
 \bigl((\mathbf x+[-R,R]^m)\times[-2Q,2Q]^n\bigr)\right)\\
 &\leqslant N_A(R,3Q).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lem-5 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Translation by $(\mathbf x,\mathbf q_0)$ preserves $u_A\mathbb Z^d$ and gives
\eqref{eq:lattice-translation-count}. If $|\mathbf q_0|\leqslant Q$, then
\[
 \mathbf q_0+[-Q,Q]^n\subseteq[-2Q,2Q]^n
 \subseteq\mathbf q_0+[-3Q,3Q]^n.
\]
Applying \eqref{eq:lattice-translation-count} to these inclusions proves
\eqref{eq:translated-count-window}.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lem-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:upper-cover}
For every $j\geqslant k$, the collection $4\mathcal G_{T_j}^{(k)}$ covers
$D_{A,T_k}(\psi)$.
<!-- end lem-6 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Let $\mathbf b\inD_{A,T_k}(\psi)$; for every $j\geqslant k$ there exists
$\mathbf q_j\in\mathbb Z^n$ such that
\begin{equation*}\label{eq:3.11}
 |\mathbf q_j|\leqslant T_j,\qquad
 |A\mathbf q_j-\mathbf b|_{\mathbb Z^m}\leqslant\psi(T_j), 
\end{equation*}
equivalently, $\mathbf b \in B(\langle A\mathbf q_j\rangle,\psi(T_j))$. We claim, inductively, that there is $B\in\mathcal G_{T_j}^{(k)}$ for which
\begin{equation}\label{eq:3.12}
 B(\langle A\mathbf q_j\rangle,\psi(T_j))\subset4B.
\end{equation}
For $j=k$, this follows from the defining property of the initial Vitali
subcollection $\mathcal G_{T_k}^{(k)}$.  Suppose \eqref{eq:3.12} holds at level $j$, with parent
$Q$ in place of $B$.  The two balls  
$B(\langle A\mathbf q_j\rangle,\psi(T_j))$ and $B(\langle A\mathbf q_{j+1}\rangle,\psi(T_{j+1}))$ both contain $\mathbf b$, and $$\operatorname{diam} B(\langle A\mathbf q_{j+1}\rangle,\psi(T_{j+1})) = 2\psi(T_{j+1})\leqslant 2\psi(T_j) = \operatorname{diam} Q.$$  Consequently,
\[
 B(\langle A\mathbf q_{j+1}\rangle,\psi(T_{j+1}))\subset6Q.
\]
The defining property of the next Vitali subcollection gives a ball $B$ at level $j+1$ for which \eqref{eq:3.12} holds.  In particular, $\mathbf b$ belongs to a member of
$4\mathcal G_{T_j}^{(k)}$ at every level.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- cor-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:cover-dimension}
For every $k\geqslant1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:3.17}
 {\dim_H}
 \bigl(D_{A,T_k}(\psi)\bigr)
 \leqslant
 {\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}
 \frac{\log\#\mathcal G_{T_\ell}^{(k)}}{-\log\psi(T_\ell)}}.
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:3.18}
 {\dim_H}\bigl(D_A(\psi)\bigr)
 \leqslant
 \sup_{k\geqslant1}\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}
 {\frac{\log\#\mathcal G_{T_\ell}^{(k)}}{-\log\psi(T_\ell)}}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end cor-2 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
By Lemma \ref{lem:upper-cover}, for every $\ell\geqslant k$ the set
$D_{A,T_k}(\psi)$ is covered by
$\#\mathcal G_{T_\ell}^{(k)}$ balls of diameter at most $8\psi(T_\ell)$.
Therefore, 
\[
    \dim_H D_{A,T_k}(\psi) \leq \underline{\dim}_B D_{A,T_k}(\psi) 
    \leq\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}\frac{\log \#\mathcal{G}_{T_\ell}^{(k)}}{-\log \psi(T_{\ell})},
    \]
where $\underline{\dim}_B$ stands for the lower box dimension.
This proves \eqref{eq:3.17}. The sets $D_{A,N}(\psi)$ are monotonically increasing, thus by \eqref{eq:3.7} one has 
$$
D_A(\psi)=\bigcup_{k\geqslant1}D_{A,T_k}(\psi).
$$
Countable stability of Hausdorff dimension now gives
\eqref{eq:3.18}.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- lem-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:upper-transition}
For $j\geqslant k$ and $Q\in\mathcal G_{T_j}^{(k)}$,
\begin{equation*}\label{eq:3.13}
 \#\mathcal G_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)
 \leqslant C_d
 \frac{\mathcal N_A(\psi(T_j),T_{j+1})}
 {\mathcal N_A(\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1})}
\end{equation*}
for some constant $C_d$ depending only on dimension.
<!-- end lem-7 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Write
\[
 Q=B(\langle A\mathbf q_0\rangle,\psi(T_j)),
 \qquad |\mathbf q_0|\leqslant T_j,
\]
and choose \(\mathbf p_0\in\mathbb Z^m\). If $\mathcal G_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q) = \varnothing$, the conclusion is immediate; from now on, assume this set to be nonempty.

Consider an arbitrary child
\[
 B=B(\langle A\mathbf q\rangle,\psi(T_{j+1}))
 \in\mathcal G_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)
\]
of $Q$, where \(|\mathbf q|\leqslant T_{j+1}\). Since $B \subseteq 6Q$ and $6 \psi(T_j) < \frac{1}{2}$, we can choose \(\mathbf p\in\mathbb Z^m\) so that
$$
B( A\mathbf q - \mathbf p,\psi(T_{j+1})) \subset B( A\mathbf q_0 - \mathbf p_0,6\psi(T_j))
$$
(here we are considering balls in $\mathbb R^m$ instead of $\mathbb T^m$). For convenience, from now on we use \(Q\) and \(B\) for the corresponding Euclidean balls $B( A\mathbf q_0 - \mathbf p_0,\psi(T_j))$ and $B( A\mathbf q - \mathbf p,\psi(T_{j+1}))$, respectively; we will refer to them as lifts of balls in $\mathbb T^m$.  Lifts of different balls in $\mathcal G_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)$ are pairwise disjoint because their
projections to \(\mathbb T^m\) are pairwise disjoint.  Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:3.14}
 \bigsqcup_{B\in\mathcal G_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)}
 \left(
 u_A\mathbb Z^d\cap
 \bigl(B\times[-2T_{j+1},2T_{j+1}]^n\bigr)
 \right)
 \subseteq
 u_A\mathbb Z^d\cap
 \bigl(6Q\times[-2T_{j+1},2T_{j+1}]^n\bigr).
\end{equation}

We first estimate the number of lattice points on the right-hand side.
Since $|\mathbf q_0|\leqslant T_j\leqslant T_{j+1}$,
\eqref{eq:translated-count-window} bounds this number by
$N_A(6\psi(T_j),3T_{j+1})$.
Lemma \ref{lem:profile-count}, followed by monotonicity and
\eqref{eq:profile-scaling}, therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:3.15}
 \#\left(
 u_A\mathbb Z^d\cap
 \bigl(6Q\times[-2T_{j+1},2T_{j+1}]^n\bigr)
 \right)
 \ll_d
 \mathcal N_A(\psi(T_j),T_{j+1}).
\end{equation}

Now fix a child
\[
 B=B(A\mathbf q-\mathbf p,\psi(T_{j+1})).
\]
Since $|\mathbf q|\leqslant T_{j+1}$, the lower bound in \eqref{eq:translated-count-window} and Lemma \ref{lem:profile-count} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:3.16}
 \#\left(
 u_A\mathbb Z^d\cap
 \bigl(B\times[-2T_{j+1},2T_{j+1}]^n\bigr)
 \right)
 \gg_d
 \mathcal N_A(\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1}).
\end{equation}

The sets on the left-hand side of \eqref{eq:3.14} are pairwise
disjoint.  Thus \eqref{eq:3.14}, \eqref{eq:3.15}, and
\eqref{eq:3.16} imply
$$
 \#\mathcal G_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)\,
 \mathcal N_A(\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1}) \ll_d
 \mathcal N_A(\psi(T_j),T_{j+1}),
$$
completing the proof.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
For $\ell\geqslant k$, Lemma \ref{lem:upper-transition} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:3.19}
 \#\mathcal G_{T_\ell}^{(k)}
 \leqslant
 \#\mathcal G_{T_k}^{(k)}C_d^{\,\ell-k}
 \prod_{j=k}^{\ell-1}
 \frac{\mathcal N_A(\psi(T_j),T_{j+1})}
 {\mathcal N_A(\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1})}.
\end{equation}
Note that $-\log\psi(T_\ell)\to\infty$,
while for each fixed $k$ the values $\#\mathcal G_{T_k}^{(k)}$ and $ \prod_{j=1}^{k-1}
 \frac{\mathcal N_A(\psi(T_j),T_{j+1})}
 {\mathcal N_A(\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1})}$ are fixed. Substituting \eqref{eq:3.19} into \eqref{eq:3.18} now gives
\eqref{eq:2.4}.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- lem-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:limit-inclusion}
If \eqref{eq:2.5} holds, then
\begin{equation*}\label{eq:3.28}
 \mathbf G_\infty^{(k)}\subseteqD_A(\rho\psi).
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lem-8 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
If $\mathbf b\in\mathbf G_\infty^{(k)}$, there are $\mathbf q_j\in\mathbb Z^n$ such that
\[
 \mathbf b\in B(\langle A\mathbf q_j\rangle,\psi(T_j)),
 \qquad |\mathbf q_j|\leqslant T_j.
\]
For $T_j\leqslant T<T_{j+1}$, condition \eqref{eq:2.5} gives
\[
 |A\mathbf q_j-\mathbf b|_{\mathbb Z^m}
 \leqslant\psi(T_j)
 \leqslant\rho\psi(T_{j+1})
 \leqslant\rho\psi(T),
 \qquad |\mathbf q_j|\leqslant T.
\]
Thus $\mathbf b \in D_A(\rho\psi)$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lem-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:mass-distribution}
Let $(X,\operatorname{dist})$ be a metric space, let $F\subseteq X$ be a Borel set, let $s>0$,
and let $\nu$ be a Borel probability measure such that
$\operatorname{supp}\nu\subseteq F$.  Suppose that there exist
$C>0$ and $r_0>0$ such that
\[
 \nu\bigl(B(x,r)\bigr)\leqslant Cr^s
\]
for every $x\in X$ and every $0<r\leqslant r_0$.  Then,
\[
 {\dim_H(F)\geqslant s}.
\]
<!-- end lem-9 -->
<!-- lem-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:tree}
Suppose that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tree-log-ratio}
 \frac{-\log\psi(T_{j+1})}{-\log\psi(T_j)}\longrightarrow1,
\end{equation}
and put
\[
 b_j^{(k)}:=
 \inf_{Q\in\mathcal G_{T_j}^{(k)}}
 \#\mathcal G_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q).
\]
Then,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:3.23}
 {\dim_H}\bigl(\mathbf G_\infty^{(k)}\bigr)
 {\geqslant}
 \liminf_{\ell\to\infty}
 {\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{j=k}^{\ell-1}\log b_j^{(k)}}
 {-\log\psi(T_\ell)}}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lem-10 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Let $\nu$ be the natural probability measure on
$\mathbf G_\infty^{(k)}$ obtained by distributing the mass of each
parent $Q \in \mathcal G_{T_j}^{(k)}$ equally among its children $B \in \mathcal G_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)$.  Every level-$\ell$ ball then has
measure at most
\[
 \left(\prod_{j=k}^{\ell-1}b_j^{(k)}\right)^{-1}.
\]
Suppose $\psi(T_\ell)\leqslant r<\psi(T_{\ell-1})$.
Pairwise disjointness of level-$\ell-1$ balls shows that a ball of radius $r$ meets at most $O_m(1)$ balls from $\mathcal G_{T_{\ell-1}}^{(k)}$.

By \eqref{eq:tree-log-ratio},
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \liminf_{\ell\to\infty}
 \frac{\sum_{j=k}^{\ell-2}\log b_j^{(k)}}{-\log\psi(T_\ell)}
 &=
 \liminf_{\ell\to\infty}
 \frac{\sum_{j=k}^{\ell-2}\log b_j^{(k)}}{-\log\psi(T_{\ell-1})}\\
 &=
 \liminf_{\ell\to\infty}
 \frac{\sum_{j=k}^{\ell-1}\log b_j^{(k)}}{-\log\psi(T_\ell)}.
\end{aligned}
\]
{If the liminf in \eqref{eq:3.23} is zero, the conclusion
is immediate. Otherwise, fix $s>0$ smaller than this liminf.}
For all sufficiently small $r$ (equivalently, sufficiently large $\ell$),
\[
 \nu(B(\mathbf x,r))
 \ll_{m}
 \left(\prod_{j=k}^{\ell-2}b_j^{(k)}\right)^{-1}
 \leqslant {\psi(T_\ell)^s}
 \leqslant {r^s}.
\]
Lemma \ref{lem:mass-distribution} gives
${\dim_H}(\mathbf G_\infty^{(k)})
 {\geqslant s}$.
Letting $s$ tend to the liminf completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lem-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:lower-transition}
For $j\geqslant k$ and $Q\in\mathcal G_{T_j}^{(k)}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:3.24}
 \#\mathcal G_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)
 \geqslant c_d
 \frac{
 \mathcal N_A(\psi(T_j)-\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1}-T_j)
 }{
 \mathcal N_A(\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1})
 }
\end{equation}
for some constant $c_d$ depending only on dimension. If \eqref{eq:2.7} holds, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:3.25}
 \#\mathcal G_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)
 \geqslant
 c_d\left(\frac{\eta-1}{\eta}\right)^d
 \frac{\mathcal N_A(\psi(T_j),T_{j+1})}
 {\mathcal N_A(\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1})}.
\end{equation}

Here we do not have to require \eqref{eq:2.5} to hold for any $\rho > 1$.
<!-- end lem-11 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Write
\[
 Q=B(\langle A\mathbf q_0\rangle,\psi(T_j)),
 \qquad |\mathbf q_0|\leqslant T_j.
\]
By maximality of $\mathcal G_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)$, every ball
\[
 B'\in\mathcal P_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)
\]
meets some ball
\[
 B\in\mathcal G_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q).
\]
Since $B$ and $B'$ have the same radius, it follows that
\[
 B'\subseteq 3B.
\]

Choose $\mathbf p_0\in\mathbb Z^m$.  From now on we use the notation $Q$ for
the Euclidean lift
\[
 Q=B(A\mathbf q_0-\mathbf p_0,\psi(T_j)) \subseteq \mathbb R^m.
\]

Since $\psi(T_j) < \frac{1}{2}$, the projection map $\mathbb R^m \to \mathbb T^m$ is injective on $Q$. Therefore, every element of $\mathcal P_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)$ has a unique lift to $\mathbb R^m$ which is contained in $Q$. Moreover, we can consider balls from $\mathcal P_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)$ and $\mathcal G_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)$ as subsets of $\mathbb R^m$; injectivity of the projection map on $Q$ yields that the triple-covering property lifts from $\mathbb T^m$ to $\mathbb R^m$.

Consider a lattice point
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lattice-pt-general-Q}
 \begin{pmatrix}
 A\mathbf q-\mathbf p\\
 \mathbf q
 \end{pmatrix}
 \in
 u_A\mathbb Z^d\cap
 \left(
 B(A\mathbf q_0-\mathbf p_0,\psi(T_j)-\psi(T_{j+1}))
 \times B(\mathbf q_0,T_{j+1}-T_j)
 \right).
\end{equation}
Then
\[
 |\mathbf q|
 \leqslant |\mathbf q-\mathbf q_0|+|\mathbf q_0|
 \leqslant T_{j+1},
\]
and
\[
 B' := B(A\mathbf q-\mathbf p,\psi(T_{j+1}))\subseteq Q,
\]
that is, $B' \in \mathcal P_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)$. The triple-covering property now implies that there exists $B \in \mathcal G_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)$ such that $B' \subseteq 3B$. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:3.24.1}
\begin{split}
 &u_A\mathbb Z^d\cap
 \left(
 B(A\mathbf q_0-\mathbf p_0,\psi(T_j)-\psi(T_{j+1}))
 \times B(\mathbf q_0,T_{j+1}-T_j)
 \right)\\
 &\quad\subseteq
 \bigcup_{B\in\mathcal G_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)}
 \left(
 u_A\mathbb Z^d\cap
 \bigl(3B\times[-T_{j+1},T_{j+1}]^n\bigr)
 \right).
\end{split}
\end{equation}

We first estimate the number of lattice points on the left-hand side.
Equation \eqref{eq:lattice-translation-count} and Lemma \ref{lem:profile-count} yield that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:3.26}
\begin{split}
 &\#\left(
 u_A\mathbb Z^d\cap
 \left(
 B(A\mathbf q_0-\mathbf p_0,\psi(T_j)-\psi(T_{j+1}))
 \times B(\mathbf q_0,T_{j+1}-T_j)
 \right)
 \right)\\
 &\quad=
 N_A\bigl(
 \psi(T_j)-\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1}-T_j
 \bigr)\\
 &\quad\gg_d
 \mathcal N_A\bigl(
 \psi(T_j)-\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1}-T_j
 \bigr).
\end{split}
\end{equation}

Now fix $B \in \mathcal G_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)$: one has
\[
 B=B(A\mathbf q-\mathbf p,\psi(T_{j+1})) \quad \text{for some} \quad
 \mathbf p\in\mathbb Z^m,\quad \mathbf q\in\mathbb Z^n,\quad |\mathbf q|\leqslant T_{j+1}.
\]
The upper bound in
\eqref{eq:translated-count-window} shows that
\[
\begin{aligned}
\#\left(u_A\mathbb Z^d\cap
 \bigl(3B\times[-T_{j+1},T_{j+1}]^n\bigr)\right)
&\leqslant
\#\left(u_A\mathbb Z^d\cap
 \bigl(3B\times[-2T_{j+1},2T_{j+1}]^n\bigr)\right)\\
&\leqslant
N_A(3\psi(T_{j+1}),3T_{j+1}).
\end{aligned}
\]
Lemma \ref{lem:profile-count} and \eqref{eq:profile-scaling} therefore give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:3.24.2}
 \#\left(
 u_A\mathbb Z^d\cap
 \bigl(3B\times[-T_{j+1},T_{j+1}]^n\bigr)
 \right)
 \ll_d
 \mathcal N_A(\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1}).
\end{equation}

The union in \eqref{eq:3.24.1} need not be disjoint, but its cardinality is at most the sum of the cardinalities of its members.  Thus \eqref{eq:3.26}
and \eqref{eq:3.24.2} imply that
\[
\begin{split}
 &\mathcal N_A\bigl(
 \psi(T_j)-\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1}-T_j
 \bigr)\\
 &\quad\ll_d
 \#\mathcal G_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)\,
 \mathcal N_A(\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1}),
\end{split}
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:3.24}.

Now suppose that \eqref{eq:2.7} holds and put
$\beta=(\eta-1)/\eta$.  Then
\begin{equation*}\label{eq:3.27}
 T_{j+1}-T_j\geqslant\beta T_{j+1},
 \qquad
 \psi(T_j)-\psi(T_{j+1})\geqslant\beta\psi(T_j).
\end{equation*}
By Lemma \ref{lem:profile-properties},
\[
\begin{split}
  \mathcal N_A(&\psi(T_j)-\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1}-T_j)\\
  &\geqslant
  \mathcal N_A(\beta\psi(T_j),\beta T_{j+1})\\
  &\geqslant\beta^d \mathcal N_A(\psi(T_j),T_{j+1}).
\end{split}
\]
Combining this with \eqref{eq:3.24} proves
\eqref{eq:3.25}.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
{Fix $k\geqslant1$.}
For any $j \geq k$ and any $Q \in \mathcal G_{T_j}^{(k)}$, the set $\mathcal G_{T_j\to T_{j+1}}^{(k)}(Q)$ is nonempty.  Thus Lemma
\ref{lem:lower-transition} implies that
\[
 b_j^{(k)}\geqslant
 \max\left\{1,
 c_d\frac{
 \mathcal N_A(\psi(T_j)-\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1}-T_j)}
 {\mathcal N_A(\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1})}
 \right\}.
\]
Lemma \ref{lem:tree} gives the corresponding lower bound for
${\dim_H}(\mathbf G_\infty^{(k)})$.  Lemma
\ref{lem:limit-inclusion} and monotonicity of Hausdorff {dimension} give
\[
 {\dim_H}\bigl(D_A(\rho\psi)\bigr)
 \geqslant {\dim_H}\bigl(\mathbf G_\infty^{(k)}\bigr),
\]
{Since $-\log\psi(T_\ell)\to\infty$, adding the first
$k-1$ logarithmic summands does not change the liminf. Thus every fixed
$k$ gives the same lower bound, proving} \eqref{eq:2.6}.  Under \eqref{eq:2.7}, use
\eqref{eq:3.25} instead to obtain \eqref{eq:2.8}.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- thm-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:uniform-pressure}
Suppose that the function $\psi$ is lower quasimultiplicative.  There exist $L_0>0$ and
numbers $\varepsilon_L\geqslant0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.5}
 \varepsilon_L=O(L^{-1})\qquad \text{as} \ L\to\infty,
\end{equation}
such that for every $L\geqslant L_0$ and $c>0$ one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.6}
 \mathcal P_{A,\psi}(L,c\mathrm e^{-L})-\varepsilon_L
 \leqslant
 \dim_HD_A(c\psi)
 \leqslant
 \mathcal P_{A,\psi}(L,c)+\varepsilon_L.
\end{equation}
The error $\varepsilon_L$ is uniform in $c$ and $A$.
<!-- end thm-6 -->
<!-- lem-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:vertical}
There is a constant $K_d\geqslant1$, depending only on $d$, such that for all $Q,R_1,R_2>0$ with
$R_1\geqslant R_2$ one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.2}
 1\leqslant\frac{\mathcal N_A(R_1,Q)}{\mathcal N_A(R_2,Q)}
 \leqslant K_d\left(\frac{R_1}{R_2}\right)^m.
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every $L>0$, $c>0$, and $j\geqslant0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.3}
 0\leqslant
 \log\frac{\mathcal N_A(c\psi(T_j),T_{j+1})}
           {\mathcal N_A(c\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1})}
 \leqslant mL+\log K_d.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lem-12 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
The first inequality in \eqref{eq:4.2} follows from the monotonicity in Lemma \ref{lem:profile-properties}.

For the second inequality, cover $[-R_1,R_1]^m$ by $\ll\left(\frac{R_1}{R_2}\right)^m$ axis-parallel cubes of side length $R_2$.  Let $\mathcal K$ be one of these cubes, and suppose $\mathcal K \times [-Q, Q]^n$ contains a point $ \mathbf v_0$ of $u_A \mathbb Z^d$. 
Since
\[
 \mathcal K\times[-Q,Q]^n
 \subseteq \mathbf v_0+\bigl([-R_2,R_2]^m\times[-2Q,2Q]^n\bigr),
\]
Lemma \ref{lem:translated-count} gives
\[
 \#\left(u_A\mathbb Z^d\cap\bigl(\mathcal K\times[-Q,Q]^n\bigr)\right)
 \leqslant N_A(R_2,2Q).
\]
Summing over these cubes and applying Lemma \ref{lem:profile-count},
monotonicity, and \eqref{eq:profile-scaling}, we obtain
\[
 \mathcal N_A(R_1,Q)
 \ll_d\left(\frac{R_1}{R_2}\right)^m N_A(R_2,2Q)
 \ll_d\left(\frac{R_1}{R_2}\right)^m\mathcal N_A(R_2,Q).
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:4.2}.  Finally, \eqref{eq:4.3} follows by taking $Q=T_{j+1}$, $R_1=c\psi(T_j)$, and $R_2=c\psi(T_{j+1})$, since \eqref{eq:1.11} gives $R_1/R_2=\mathrm e^L$.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
For $c > 0$, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:R_j(c)}
 \mathcal R_j(c):=
 \frac{\mathcal N_A(c\psi(T_j),T_{j+1})}
      {\mathcal N_A(c\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1})}.
\end{equation}

Fix $\tau$ and $R_0$ as in Definition \ref{def:regular}.
Choose $L_0>\max\{\tau\log R_0,\log R_0\}$.
Fix $L\geqslant L_0$ and $c>0$. We apply both Propositions \ref{prop:upper} and \ref{prop:lower} to
$c\psi$. By \eqref{eq:1.3} and \eqref{eq:1.11}, for all sufficiently
large $j$ one has
\[
 \psi(\mathrm e^{L/\tau}T_j)\geqslant\mathrm e^{-L}\psi(T_j)=\psi(T_{j+1}).
\]
Strict monotonicity of $\psi$ therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.13}
 T_{j+1}\geqslant \mathrm e^{L/\tau}T_j\geqslant R_0T_j.
\end{equation}
Moreover, \eqref{eq:1.11} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.11}
 \frac{c\psi(T_j)}{{c\psi(T_{j+1})}}
 =\mathrm e^L\geqslant R_0.
\end{equation}
Thus \eqref{eq:2.5} holds with $\rho=\mathrm e^L$, and
\eqref{eq:2.7} holds with the fixed constant
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.14}
 \eta:=R_0.
\end{equation}
We omit finitely many initial mesh points and modify
$c\psi$ on the resulting bounded initial interval to satisfy the
standing smallness assumption in Section \ref{sec:CD}. This changes
neither $D_A(c\psi)$ and $D_A(\mathrm e^Lc\psi)$ nor the tail limits below.

First, we obtain the upper estimate. Using \eqref{eq:R_j(c)} and \eqref{eq:1.11}, Proposition \ref{prop:upper} {and \eqref{eq:1.13.1}} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.9}
\begin{split}
 \dim_HD_A({c\psi})
 &\leqslant
 {\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}
 \frac{(\ell-1)\log C_d+
       \sum_{j=1}^{\ell-1}\log\mathcal R_j(c)}
      {\ell L-\log c}}\\
 &{=}
 \mathcal P_{A,\psi}(L,{c})+\frac{\log C_d}{L}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}

For the lower estimate, apply estimate \eqref{eq:2.8} of Proposition
\ref{prop:lower} to $c\psi$. Put
\[
 c_*:=c_d\left(\frac{\eta-1}{\eta}\right)^d.
\]
Proposition \ref{prop:lower} gives
\[
 \dim_HD_A(\mathrm e^Lc\psi)
 \geqslant
 \liminf_{\ell\to\infty}
 \frac{\sum_{j=1}^{\ell-1}\log\max\{1,c_*\mathcal R_j(c)\}}
      {\ell L-\log c}
 \geqslant \mathcal P_{A,\psi}(L,c)+\frac{\log c_*}{L}.
\]
Here the last inequality follows from
$\log\max\{1,c_*\mathcal R_j(c)\}\geqslant\log c_*+\log\mathcal R_j(c)$
and \eqref{eq:1.13.1}. Replacing $c$ by $c\mathrm e^{-L}$ in this lower
bound and combining it with \eqref{eq:4.9} proves \eqref{eq:4.6} with
\[
 \varepsilon_L=
 \frac1L\max\left\{\log C_d,
 {0,-\log c_*}\right\}.
\]
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- cor-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:endpoints}
For every $L\geqslant L_0$, where $L_0$ is as in Theorem \ref{thm:uniform-pressure},
\begin{equation*}\label{eq:4.21}
\begin{split}
 \left|
 {\inf_{c>0}\mathcal P_{A,\psi}(L,c)}
 -{\inf_{c>0}\dim_HD_A(c\psi)}
 \right|&\leqslant\varepsilon_L,\\
 \left|
 {\sup_{c>0}\mathcal P_{A,\psi}(L,c)}
 -{\sup_{c>0}\dim_HD_A(c\psi)}
 \right|&\leqslant\varepsilon_L.
\end{split}
\end{equation*}
Consequently, the limits $P_-(A,\psi)$ and $P_+(A,\psi)$ introduced
in Theorem \ref{thm:main} exist, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.22}
\begin{split}
 P_-(A,\psi)
 &={\inf_{c>0}\dim_HD_A(c\psi)}
 =\inf_{c>0}\dim_HD_A(\widetilde\psi_c),\\
 P_+(A,\psi)
 &={\sup_{c>0}\dim_HD_A(c\psi)}
 =\sup_{c>0}\dim_HD_A(\widetilde\psi_c).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
<!-- end cor-3 -->
<!-- prop-12: body_md -->
\label{prop:no-drop}
Under the assumptions of Theorem \ref{thm:main},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.28}
 \dim_H{\mathrm{Sing}}_A(\psi)
 =\inf_{c>0}\dim_HD_A(\widetilde\psi_c)
 =\inf_{c>0}\dim_HD_A(c\psi)
 =P_-(A,\psi).
\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-12 -->
<!-- lem-13: body_md -->
\label{lem:diagonal}
Let $d_0 \in \mathbb R$, and let
\[
 a_j^{(p)}\in[0,M], \qquad j\in\mathbb Z_{\geqslant0},\quad p\in\mathbb N,
\]
satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.23}
 \liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac1N
 \sum_{j=0}^{N-1}a_j^{(p)}
 \geqslant d_0\qquad \text{for any} \  p\in\mathbb N.
\end{equation}
Then there is a sequence $\{p_j\}_{j\geqslant0}$ of positive integers such
that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.24}
 p_{j+1}-p_j\in\{0,1\},\qquad
 p_j\to\infty,\qquad
 \frac{p_j}{j}\to0,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.25}
 \liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac1N
 \sum_{j=0}^{N-1}a_j^{(p_j)}
 \geqslant d_0.
\end{equation}
The same assertion holds if both of the inequalities in
\eqref{eq:4.23} and \eqref{eq:4.25} reversed.
<!-- end lem-13 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Choose a decreasing sequence
 $\{\delta_p\}_{p\in \mathbb N}$ of positive real numbers such that $\delta_p\to0$ and put
\[
 B_p(N):=
 \sum_{j=0}^{N-1}
 \bigl(a_j^{(p)}-(d_0-\delta_p)\bigr),
 \qquad N\in\mathbb Z_{\geqslant0},\quad p\in\mathbb N.
\]
For every fixed $p\in\mathbb N$, equation \eqref{eq:4.23} implies that $\liminf\limits_{N \to \infty} \frac{B_p(N)}{N} \geq \delta_p$ and thus
\[
 B_p(N)\geqslant\frac{\delta_p}{2}N
\]
for all sufficiently large $N$. In particular, $B_p(N)\to+\infty$.

We recursively choose integers
\[
 1\leqslant K_1<K_2<\cdots
\]
as follows.  Put $K_0=0$.  Having chosen $K_{p-1}$, choose an integer
\[
 R_p>\max\{K_{p-1},p^2\},
\]
and let $K_p\geqslant R_p$ be a point at which $B_p$ attains its minimum on
the integers $N\geqslant R_p$.  Such a point exists because
$B_p(N)\to+\infty$.  For every $N\geqslant K_p$, we then have
$B_p(N)-B_p(K_p)\geqslant0$,
and consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.26}
 \sum_{j=K_p}^{N-1}a_j^{(p)}
 \geqslant(d_0-\delta_p)(N-K_p).
\end{equation}

Put $p_j=1$ for $0\leqslant j<K_1$, and define $p_j=p$ when
\[
 K_p\leqslant j<K_{p+1}, \qquad p\in\mathbb N.
\]
The first two assertions in \eqref{eq:4.24} are immediate. Moreover, if
$K_p\leqslant j<K_{p+1}$, then
\[
 0\leqslant\frac{p_j}{j}
 =\frac pj
 \leqslant\frac p{K_p}
 <\frac1p.
\]
Since $p\to\infty$ as $j\to\infty$, this proves the last assertion in
\eqref{eq:4.24}.


For $N\geqslant K_1$, applying \eqref{eq:4.26} to each completed block and
the last partial block gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.27}
 \sum_{j=0}^{N-1}a_j^{(p_j)}
\geqslant d_0N-\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\delta_{p_j}+O(1).
\end{equation}
Here the $O(1)$ term covers the fixed initial block $0\leqslant j<K_1$.  Since $p_j\to\infty$ and $\delta_p\to0$,
\[
 \frac1N\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\delta_{p_j}\longrightarrow0
\]
as $N \to \infty$. Dividing \eqref{eq:4.27} by $N$ and taking the lower limit proves
\eqref{eq:4.25}.

For the reversed inequalities, choose  $K_1=0$ and
recursively choose integers $K_{p+1}>\max\{K_p,(p+1)^2\}$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reverse_ineq_c}
 \frac{MK_p+\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{K_{p+1}-1}a_j^{(p)}}
      {K_{p+1}}
 \leqslant d_0+\frac1p.
\end{equation}
This is possible by the reversed hypothesis \eqref{eq:4.23}.
Define $p_j$ by the same block rule as above. Then
\eqref{eq:4.24} holds as before, while boundedness and nonnegativity of $a_j^{(p)}$ toogether with \eqref{eq:reverse_ineq_c} give
\[
 \sum_{j=0}^{K_{p+1}-1}a_j^{(p_j)}
 \leqslant MK_p+\sum_{j=0}^{K_{p+1}-1}a_j^{(p)}
 \leqslant\left(d_0+\frac1p\right)K_{p+1}.
\]
Dividing by $K_{p+1}$ and letting $p\to\infty$ proves the reversed
\eqref{eq:4.25}.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- lem-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:interpolation}
Let $T_j\to\infty$ be strictly increasing and let $c_j\to\infty$ be
nondecreasing and positive. Suppose that, for some $0<\delta<1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:interpolation-condition}
 1\leqslant\frac{c_{j+1}}{c_j}
 \leqslant\left(\frac{\psi(T_j)}{\psi(T_{j+1})}\right)^\delta
 \qquad\text{for every }j.
\end{equation}
Then there exist continuous strictly decreasing functions $\varphi$
and $\vartheta$, tending to zero, such that
\begin{equation*}\label{eq:interpolation-values}
 \varphi(T_j)=c_j\psi(T_j),
 \qquad
 \vartheta(T_j)=c_j^{-1}\psi(T_j),
\end{equation*}
and $\psi=o(\varphi)$, $\vartheta=o(\psi)$. The ratios
$\varphi/\psi$ and $\psi/\vartheta$ are nondecreasing.
If $\psi$ is lower quasimultiplicative, then so are $\varphi$
and $\vartheta$.
<!-- end lem-14 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
For $T_j\leqslant T\leqslant T_{j+1}$, put
\[
 \beta_j:=
 \frac{\log(c_{j+1}/c_j)}
      {\log(\psi(T_j)/\psi(T_{j+1}))}\in[0,\delta],
 \qquad
 c(T):=c_j\left(\frac{\psi(T_j)}{\psi(T)}\right)^{\beta_j},
\]
and extend $c$ constantly to the omitted initial interval.
Then $c$ is continuous and nondecreasing, with $c(T_j)=c_j$.
Define
\[
 \varphi:=c\psi,\qquad \vartheta:=c^{-1}\psi.
\]
On each mesh interval, these functions are positive constant multiples of
$\psi^{1-\beta_j}$ and $\psi^{1+\beta_j}$, respectively.

For $T_j \leqslant T \leqslant S \leqslant T_{j+1}$, the definition shows that $\frac{c(S)}{c(T)}
=\left(\frac{\psi(T)}{\psi(S)}\right)^{\beta_j}$. Therefore, for any $S \geqslant T$ one has
$$
1 \leqslant \frac{c(S)}{c(T)} \leqslant \left(\frac{\psi(T)}{\psi(S)}\right)^{\delta}
$$
and so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:interpolation-comparison}
\begin{split}
 \frac{\psi(S)}{\psi(T)}
 &\leqslant\frac{\varphi(S)}{\varphi(T)}
 \leqslant\left(\frac{\psi(S)}{\psi(T)}\right)^{1-\delta},\\
 \left(\frac{\psi(S)}{\psi(T)}\right)^{1+\delta}
 &\leqslant\frac{\vartheta(S)}{\vartheta(T)}
 \leqslant\frac{\psi(S)}{\psi(T)}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Thus both functions are strictly decreasing to zero. Since
$c(T)\nearrow \infty$, we get the relative asymptotics of the new functions compared to $\psi$ and monotonicity of ratios. Finally, \eqref{eq:interpolation-comparison} and \eqref{eq:1.3} give lower
quasimultiplicativity with exponents $\tau$ and $(1+\delta)\tau$,
respectively.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
The inclusion
{${\mathrm{Sing}}_A(\psi)\subseteqD_A(c\psi)$ for every $c>0$}, together
with \eqref{eq:4.22}, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.29}
 \dim_H{\mathrm{Sing}}_A(\psi)\leqslant P_-(A,\psi).
\end{equation}
We prove the reverse inequality.  The case $P_-(A,\psi)=0$ is
immediate, so assume $P_-(A,\psi)>0$.  Fix $s$ with $0<s<P_-(A,\psi)$.

Fix $\tau$ and $R_0$ as in Definition \ref{def:regular}. As in \eqref{eq:4.14}, let $\eta=R_0$, and put
\[
 c_*:=c_d\left(\frac{\eta-1}{\eta}\right)^d,
 \qquad
 \delta_L:=\frac{\max\{0,-\log c_*\}}{L}.
\]
Due to the definition given in \eqref{eq:pressure-minus}, we can choose $L$ so large that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:choice-of-L}
 s<{\inf_{c>0}\mathcal P_{A,\psi}(L,c)}-\delta_L.
\end{equation}
Fix such an $L>1$.  For $p\geqslant1$ and $j\geqslant0$, let
\begin{equation*}\label{eq:4.30}
 a_j^{(p)}:=\frac1L
 \log
 \frac{\mathcal N_A(\mathrm e^{-p}\psi(T_j),T_{j+1})}
      {\mathcal N_A(\mathrm e^{-p}\psi(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1})}.
\end{equation*}
By Lemma \ref{lem:vertical},
$$
 a_j^{(p)}\in\left[0,m+\frac{\log K_d}{L}\right].
$$
By \eqref{eq:4.19},
\[
 \liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac1N
 \sum_{j=0}^{N-1}a_j^{(p)}
 =
 \mathcal P_{A,\psi}(L,{\mathrm e^{-p}})
 \geqslant{\inf_{c>0}\mathcal P_{A,\psi}(L,c)}.
\]
Apply Lemma \ref{lem:diagonal} with {$d_0=\inf\limits_{c>0}\mathcal P_{A,\psi}(L,c)$}, and let $\{p_j\}$ be
the resulting sequence.  Then
{
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.31}
 \liminf_{N\to\infty}
\frac1N\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}a_j^{(p_j)}
 \geqslant\inf_{c>0}\mathcal P_{A,\psi}(L,c).
\end{equation}
}

By \eqref{eq:1.11} and \eqref{eq:4.24}, the sequences $\{T_j\}$ and
$c_j:=\mathrm e^{p_j}$ satisfy \eqref{eq:interpolation-condition} with
$\delta=L^{-1}$. Let $\vartheta$ be the continuous strictly decreasing,
lower quasimultiplicative function given by Lemma
\ref{lem:interpolation}; one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.33}
 \frac{\vartheta(T)}{\psi(T)}
 \longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
At the mesh points,
{
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.34}
 \vartheta(T_j)=\mathrm e^{-p_j}\psi(T_j)=\mathrm e^{-(jL+p_j)}
\end{equation}
}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.35}
 \frac{\vartheta(T_j)}{\vartheta(T_{j+1})}
 =\mathrm e^{L+p_{j+1}-p_j}\in[\mathrm e^L,\mathrm e^{L+1}].
\end{equation}
We can additionally assume that $L \geq L_0$, where $L_0$ is chosen as in the proof of Theorem \ref{thm:uniform-pressure}. 
After omitting finitely many initial indices, \eqref{eq:4.13} gives
\[
 T_{j+1}\geqslant R_0T_j.
\]
Moreover, since $L$ is sufficiently large that $\mathrm e^L\geqslant R_0$, the
lower bound in \eqref{eq:4.35} gives
\[
 \vartheta(T_j)\geqslant R_0\vartheta(T_{j+1}).
\]
Thus \eqref{eq:2.7} holds for $\vartheta$ with
$\eta=R_0$.  The upper bound in \eqref{eq:4.35} shows that
\eqref{eq:2.5} holds with
\[
 \rho_L:=\mathrm e^{L+1}.
\]
As before, $\vartheta$ may be modified on the omitted bounded interval
to satisfy the standing smallness assumption in Section \ref{sec:CD}. Now we can apply Proposition \ref{prop:lower} to the function $\vartheta$ and sequence $\{ T_j \}$.

By \eqref{eq:4.34} and since $p_{j+1}\geqslant p_j$, one has
$\vartheta(T_{j+1})\leqslant\mathrm e^{-p_j}\psi(T_{j+1})$. Thus monotonicity
in \eqref{eq:4.2} gives
\[
 \log\max\left\{1,c_*
 \frac{\mathcal N_A(\vartheta(T_j),T_{j+1})}
      {\mathcal N_A(\vartheta(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1})}\right\}
 \geqslant La_j^{(p_j)}+\log c_*.
\]
Proposition \ref{prop:lower} and \eqref{eq:4.34} therefore give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:4.40}
\begin{gathered}
 \dim_HD_A(\rho_L\vartheta)
 \geqslant\liminf_{N\to\infty}
 \frac{L\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}a_j^{(p_j)}+(N-1)\log c_*}{NL+p_N}\\
 =\liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac1N\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}a_j^{(p_j)}
   +\frac{\log c_*}{L}
 \geqslant\inf_{c>0}\mathcal P_{A,\psi}(L,c)-\delta_L>s.
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
The equality uses boundedness of $a_j^{(p_j)}$ and $p_N/N\to0$
from \eqref{eq:4.24}; the last line follows from \eqref{eq:4.31},
the definition of $\delta_L$, and \eqref{eq:choice-of-L}.

By \eqref{eq:4.33}, for every $c>0$ one has
$\rho_L\vartheta(T)\leqslant c\psi(T)$ for all sufficiently large $T$.
Therefore
\begin{equation*}\label{eq:4.41}
 D_A(\rho_L\vartheta)
 \subseteq
 \bigcap_{c>0}D_A(c\psi)
 ={\mathrm{Sing}}_A(\psi),
\end{equation*}
and thus
$$
 \dim_H{\mathrm{Sing}}_A(\psi)\geqslant s.
$$
Letting $s\nearrow P_-(A,\psi)$ proves the reverse inequality in
\eqref{eq:4.29}.  The remaining equalities in \eqref{eq:4.28} follow
from \eqref{eq:4.22}.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lem-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:power-profile}
The function $F$ is continuous and locally piecewise affine, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-profile-comparison}
 F(x,y)=\log\mathcal N_A(\mathrm e^{-y},\mathrm e^x)+O_d(1),
 \qquad 0\leqslant-\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}\leqslant m
\end{equation}
wherever the derivative exists.
<!-- end lem-15 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Minkowski's second theorem, applied in the span of a rank-$r$
sublattice $a_{x,y}\Gamma$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Mink_second_first}
 |a_{x,y}\omega_\Gamma|
 \gg_d\prod_{i=1}^r\lambda_i(a_{x,y}\Lambda_A).
\end{equation}
Taking $\Gamma$ to be the primitive sublattice of $\Lambda_A$ in the span of independent vectors whose images under $a_{x,y}$ realize the first $r$ minima yields the reverse bound.
Now let 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-small-lattice}
 r':=\#\{i:\lambda_i(a_{x,y}\Lambda_A)<1\}, \quad
 \Gamma_{r'}:=\Lambda_A\cap
 \operatorname{span}_{\mathbb R}\{\mathbf v\in\Lambda_A:|a_{x,y}\mathbf v|<1\}.
\end{equation}
The discussion above shows that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Mink_second_equiv}
 \min_{\Gamma}|a_{x,y}\omega_\Gamma| \asymp_d |a_{x,y}\omega_{\Gamma_{r'}}|
 \asymp_d\prod_{i=1}^{r'}\lambda_i(a_{x,y}\Lambda_A).
\end{equation}
It remains to use \eqref{eq:rectangular-profile} to deduce
the first assertion in \eqref{eq:power-profile-comparison}.



For a fixed $\Gamma$ of rank $r$, write $$\omega_\Gamma=\sum_{|I|=r}\omega_I e_I,
\qquad
e_I=e_{i_1}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{i_r}.$$
If \(I\) contains \(a\) indices from the first \(m\) coordinates and \(b\) from the last \(n\), then \(a+b=r\), and 
\[
a_{x,y}e_I=\mathrm e^{ay-bx}e_I.
\]
Therefore, 
$$
|a_{x,y}\omega_\Gamma| =\max_{\substack{a+b=r\\0\leqslant a\leqslant m,\;0\leqslant b\leqslant n}} C_a\,\mathrm e^{ay-bx}
$$
for some $C_a \geqslant 0$, and thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-exterior-profile}
 \log|a_{x,y}\omega_\Gamma|
 =\max_{a+b=r}\{c_a+ay-bx\},
 \qquad 0\leqslant a\leqslant m,\quad0\leqslant b\leqslant n.
\end{equation}
Here $c_a = \log C_a \in\mathbb R\cup\{-\infty\}$ depends on $\Gamma$.
We have established earlier that $F(x,y) \geqslant 0$, and on any compact set of $(x,y)$ only finitely many primitive exterior vectors can satisfy
$|a_{x,y}\omega_\Gamma|\leqslant1$ at some point, so the maximum defining
$F$ is locally finite. Formula~\eqref{eq:power-exterior-profile}
therefore proves continuity, local piecewise affinity, and the
slope bound in \eqref{eq:power-profile-comparison}.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lem-16: body_md -->
\label{lem:power-gaps}
Let $1\leqslant r<d$. On each interval where $g_r<g_{r+1}$,
the vectors in $\Lambda_A$ whose images realize the first $r$
minima span a fixed subspace, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-gap-profile}
 G_r(x)=\max_{a+b=r}\{c_a+(a\tau-b)x\}+O_d(1),
 \qquad 0\leqslant a\leqslant m,\quad0\leqslant b\leqslant n,
\end{equation}
with constants $c_a\in\mathbb R\cup\{-\infty\}$ depending on this span.
For $r=0$ and $r=d$, the spans are always $\{0\}$ and $\mathbb R^d$,
respectively, and \eqref{eq:power-gap-profile} holds on every
interval, with no inequality between successive minima required.
<!-- end lem-16 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
On an interval where $g_r<g_{r+1}$, the span is locally constant and hence constant. If $\Gamma$ is the primitive lattice in this
span, the minima of $a_{x,\tau x}\Gamma$ are precisely
$\mathrm e^{g_1},\ldots,\mathrm e^{g_r}$. Minkowski's second theorem and
\eqref{eq:power-exterior-profile} prove
\eqref{eq:power-gap-profile}.
For $r=0$, the empty sum gives $G_0=0$. For $r=d$,
unimodularity of $\Lambda_A$ and Minkowski's second theorem give
$G_d(x)=(m\tau-n)x+O_d(1)$ for every $x$.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- lem-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:power-maximizer}
Let $r'$ and $\Gamma_{r'}$ be as in \eqref{eq:power-small-lattice}. There is $H_d>0$ such that, if every logarithm of successive minimum of $a_{x,y}\Lambda_A$ lies outside $[-H_d,H_d]$, then the maximum
defining $F(x,y)$ is attained uniquely by $\Gamma_{r'}$.
<!-- end lem-17 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Recall that, by \eqref{eq:Mink_second_first} and \eqref{eq:Mink_second_equiv}, 
\[
\begin{aligned}
 |a_{x,y}\omega_{\Gamma_{r'}}|
 \asymp_d\prod_{i=1}^{r'}\lambda_i(a_{x,y}\Lambda_A), \quad
 |a_{x,y}\omega_\Gamma|
 \gg_d\prod_{i=1}^r\lambda_i(a_{x,y}\Lambda_A)
 \quad\text{if }\operatorname{rank}\Gamma=r.
\end{aligned}
\]
For $r\ne r'$, the latter product exceeds the former by a factor at least $\mathrm e^{H_d}$. If $0<r'<d$ and $\Gamma\ne\Gamma_{r'}$ has rank
$r'$, the last minimum of $a_{x,y}\Gamma$ is at least
$\lambda_{r'+1}(a_{x,y}\Lambda_A)$: otherwise its span
would equal that of $a_{x,y}\Gamma_{r'}$, and primitivity would give
$\Gamma=\Gamma_{r'}$. Minkowski's second theorem therefore gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
 |a_{x,y}\omega_\Gamma|
 \gg_d\lambda_{r+1}(a_{x,y}\Lambda_A)
            \prod_{i=1}^{r-1}\lambda_i(a_{x,y}\Lambda_A)
 >\mathrm e^{2H_d}\prod_{i=1}^r\lambda_i(a_{x,y}\Lambda_A).
\end{aligned}
\]
Taking $H_d$ large enough to suppress absolute constants arising from applications of Minkowski's second theorem proves uniqueness. For $r=0,d$, there is no other primitive sublattice of the same rank, so only the different-rank comparison is needed.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- lem-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:power-threshold}
If $\tau\notin\mathcal E_{m,n}$, then, for every $1\leqslant i\leqslant d$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-single-threshold-density}
 \lim_{\eta\searrow0}\limsup_{x\to\infty}\frac1x
 \left|\left\{u\in[0,x]:|g_i(u)|\leqslant\eta x\right\}\right|=0.
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-threshold-density}
 \lim_{\eta\searrow0}\limsup_{x\to\infty}\frac1x
 \left|\left\{u\in[0,x]:\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant d}|g_i(u)|\leqslant\eta x
 \right\}\right|=0.
\end{equation}
In particular, replacing $\eta x$ by any fixed constant gives
a set of length $o(x)$.
<!-- end lem-18 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Fix $1\leqslant i\leqslant d$. Each $g_k$ is Lipschitz with constant
$K:=\max\{1,\tau\}$: changing $u$ by $v\geqslant0$ multiplies the norm
of any vector by a factor between $\mathrm e^{-v}$ and $\mathrm e^{\tau v}$.

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{Step 1. Bound the length of the set where certain sums of minima are close.}
Let $0\leqslant r<s\leqslant d$, and suppose that \begin{equation}\label{eq:k_non-bdry_comparison}
    g_k(u)<g_{k+1}(u)
\ \text{for any} \ u \in I \ \text{and every} \
k\in\{r,s\}\cap\{1,\ldots,d-1\}.
\end{equation}
Recall that $G_0=0$.  
By Lemma~\ref{lem:power-gaps}, the function $G_s-G_r$ differs
by $O_d(1)$ from a continuous piecewise-affine function with at
most $2d+1$ pieces. Its slopes have the form
\[
 a\tau-b,\qquad a+b=s-r>0,\qquad
 a,b\in\mathbb Z,\quad |a|\leqslant m,\quad |b|\leqslant n.
\]
A zero slope would imply $a,b>0$ and
$\tau=b/a\in\mathcal E_{m,n}$. Thus the finitely many possible
slopes are bounded away from zero in terms of $m,n,\tau$.
On each affine piece, the inverse image of an interval of
length $O_d(H+1)$ therefore has length $O_{m,n,\tau}(H+1)$.
It follows that, for every $H\geqslant0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-separated-block}
 \left|\left\{u\in I:|G_s(u)-G_r(u)|\leqslant H\right\}\right|
 \ll_{m,n,\tau}H+1.
\end{equation}

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{Step 2. Decomposing the desired set into a finite union.}
Fix $x\geqslant1$ and $0<\eta<4^{-d}$, and put
\[
 R_j:=\eta^{(d-j)/d}x,
 \qquad 0\leqslant j\leqslant d.
\]
At any point $u$ with $|g_i(u)|\leqslant\eta x=R_0$, the $d+1$
nested sets
\begin{equation}\label{eq: set-of-indices-Rj}
 \{k:|g_k(u)|\leqslant R_j\},\qquad 0\leqslant j\leqslant d,
\end{equation}
all contain $i$. Two consecutive sets must coincide, since their cardinalities cannot increase strictly $d$ times. Since $g_1(u)\leqslant\cdots\leqslant g_d(u)$, every index between two indices satisfying $-R_j\leqslant g_k(u)\leqslant R_j$ also satisfies these inequalities. Denote the first and last indices in \eqref{eq: set-of-indices-Rj} by $r+1$ and $s$, respectively; the set \eqref{eq: set-of-indices-Rj} is therefore equal to $\{r+1,\ldots,s\}$, and $0\leqslant r<i\leqslant s\leqslant d$ because it contains $i$.
For $0\leqslant j<d$ and $0\leqslant r<i\leqslant s\leqslant d$, let $E_{j,r,s}$
be the set of points $u\in[0,x]$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-block-separation}
\begin{gathered}
 |g_k(u)|\leqslant R_j \ \text{if}\ r<k\leqslant s,\\
 g_r(u)<-R_{j+1} \ \text{if } \ r\geqslant1, \qquad 
 g_{s+1}(u)>R_{j+1} \ \text{if } \ s<d.
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
The preceding argument proves that
\[
 \{u\in[0,x]:|g_i(u)|\leqslant\eta x\}
 \subseteq\bigcup_{\substack{0\leqslant j<d\\0\leqslant r<i\leqslant s\leqslant d}}E_{j,r,s}.
\]

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{Step 3. Estimate for lengths of the sets $E_{j,r,s}$.}
Fix $j,r,s$ and partition $[0,x]$ into intervals of length $R_{j+1}/(8K)$ (the last one may be shorter). If an interval $I$ meets $E_{j,r,s}$ at $u_0$, then $$\begin{aligned}
g_{r+1}(u_0)-g_r(u_0)&>R_{j+1}-R_j
&&\text{if }r\geqslant1,\\
g_{s+1}(u_0)-g_s(u_0)&>R_{j+1}-R_j
&&\text{if }s<d.
\end{aligned}$$
Lipschitz continuity then guarantees that 
$$\begin{gathered}
g_{r+1}(u)-g_r(u)>R_{j+1}-R_j-\frac{R_{j+1}}4 > 0
\ \text{if }r\geqslant1,\\
g_{s+1}(u)-g_s(u)>R_{j+1}-R_j-\frac{R_{j+1}}4 > 0
\ \text{if }s<d
\end{gathered}$$
for any $u\in I$; thus \eqref{eq:k_non-bdry_comparison} holds.
Moreover, \eqref{eq:power-block-separation} gives $|G_s-G_r|\leqslant dR_j$ on $E_{j,r,s}$. Applying
\eqref{eq:power-separated-block}, we conclude that
\[
 |E_{j,r,s}\cap I|\ll_{m,n,\tau}R_j+1.
\]
There are $O_\tau(x/R_{j+1})$ intervals in the partition , since $R_{j+1}\leqslant x$.
Using $R_j=\eta^{1/d}R_{j+1}$ and
$R_{j+1}\geqslant\eta^{(d-1)/d}x$, we obtain
\[
 |E_{j,r,s}|
 \ll_{m,n,\tau}\frac{x}{R_{j+1}}(R_j+1)
 \leqslant \eta^{1/d}x+\eta^{-(d-1)/d}.
\]
Summing over the finitely many choices in Step~2 gives
\[
 \left|\left\{u\in[0,x]:|g_i(u)|\leqslant\eta x\right\}\right|
 \ll_{m,n,\tau}\eta^{1/d}x+\eta^{-(d-1)/d}.
\]
Dividing by $x$, then letting $x\to\infty$ and
$\eta\searrow0$, proves \eqref{eq:power-single-threshold-density}.
Taking the union over $i=1,\ldots,d$ proves
\eqref{eq:power-threshold-density}. The last assertion holds trivially.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- lem-19: body_md -->
\label{lem:power-pressure}
Suppose that $\tau\notin\mathcal E_{m,n}$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-pressure-integral}
 \mathcal P_{A,\psi_\tau}(L,c)
 =\liminf_{x\to\infty}\frac{B(x)}{\tau x}+O_d(L^{-1})
\end{equation}
for every $L,c>0$, with an implied
constant independent of $c$.
<!-- end lem-19 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Fix $L,c>0$, put $w:=\log c$, and choose
$R>|w|$. For $\psi=\psi_\tau$, equation~\eqref{eq:1.11} gives
$T_j=\mathrm e^{jL/\tau}$. Put $x_j=jL/\tau$ and consider the rectangles
\begin{equation}\label{eq:profile-rectangles}
 [x_j,x_{j+1}]\times[jL-R,(j+1)L+R],\qquad j\geqslant0.
\end{equation}
Call a mesh interval $[x_j,x_{j+1}]$ bad if some $|g_i|$ is at most
$H_d+2(L+R+1)\max\{1,\tau,\tau^{-1}\}$ at some point of it.
Lipschitz continuity and Lemma~\ref{lem:power-threshold} show that
there are $o(N)$ bad intervals among the first $N$: the union of
such intervals is contained in a fixed, slightly wider threshold
strip. Call each remaining mesh interval good. A \emph{good block}
is a maximal union of consecutive good mesh intervals. Among the
first $N$ mesh intervals, the good intervals form at most one
more block than there are bad intervals, hence $o(N)+1$ good blocks.

Fix a good block and let $r'$ and $\Gamma_{r'}$ be defined by
\eqref{eq:power-small-lattice} at $(x,\tau x)$; namely, $r'=\#\{i:g_i(x)<0\}$. The rank $r'$ is constant throughout the block, since all the $|g_i|$ are separated from zero. Thus $\Gamma_{r'}$ is fixed by Lemma~\ref{lem:power-gaps}.

If $(x,y)$ belongs to a rectangle  \eqref{eq:profile-rectangles}, then $|y-\tau x|\leqslant L+R$.
Choose a rectangle corresponding to an interval from our fixed good block, and consider the lattice $a_{x,y} \Lambda_A$ instead of $a_{x, \tau x} \Lambda_A$. Application of $a_{x,y}a_{x, \tau x}^{-1}$ multiplies each vector norm by a factor between $\mathrm e^{-(L+R)}$ and $\mathrm e^{L+R}$. Since the interval to which $x$ belongs is good, the first $r'$ minimizing vectors remain shorter than one and the number of minima less than one remains $r'$.
Thus the rank and lattice in \eqref{eq:power-small-lattice}
remain $r'$ and $\Gamma_{r'}$, and all logarithmic minima remain
outside $[-H_d,H_d]$. Lemma~\ref{lem:power-maximizer} and
\eqref{eq:power-exterior-profile} therefore give
\[
 F(x,y)= -\log|a_{x,y}\omega_{\Gamma_{r'}}| = - \max_{a+b=r'}\{c_a+ay-bx\} =r'x-\max_a\{c_a+a(x+y)\}.
\]
The affine branch of the maximum changes at at most $d$ lines of form $x + y = const$.
On the $j$th rectangle, $x+y$ ranges from
$(1+\tau^{-1})jL-R$ to $(1+\tau^{-1})(j+1)L+R$.
Each branch change line $x+y = const$ therefore meets only $O_{L,R,\tau}(1)$ rectangles.
Apart from these $O_{L,R,\tau,d}(1)$ exceptions per block (summing to $o(N)$ exceptional rectangles overall), $F$ has
constant vertical slope on each rectangle. Including all rectangles
over bad intervals, the total number of exceptional rectangles is $o(N)$.

On a nonexceptional rectangle with vertical slope $-a_j$, 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-pressure-transition}
\begin{gathered}
 \log\frac{\mathcal N_A(c\psi_\tau(T_j),T_{j+1})}
              {\mathcal N_A(c\psi_\tau(T_{j+1}),T_{j+1})}
 ={}F(x_{j+1},jL-w)\\
 -F(x_{j+1},(j+1)L-w)+O_d(1)
 ={}B(x_{j+1})-B(x_j)+O_d(1).
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
Indeed: both arguments of $F$ belong to the rectangle, so the difference of these terms equals $a_jL$, which is$B(x_{j+1})-B(x_j)$.
The first equality uses
\eqref{eq:power-profile-comparison}.

\smallskip

On an exceptional rectangle, the vertical increment of $F$ and
the increment of $B$ both lie in $[0,mL]$, by the slope bound in
\eqref{eq:power-profile-comparison}. The $o(N)$ exceptions therefore
contribute a total error $o(NL)$. Summing
\eqref{eq:power-pressure-transition}, dividing by $NL$, and using
\eqref{eq:4.19}, we obtain
\[
 \mathcal P_{A,\psi_\tau}(L,c)
 =\liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac{B(x_N)}{NL}+O_d(L^{-1}).
\]
The implied constant is independent of $c$, since it comes from
the uniform error in \eqref{eq:power-profile-comparison}. 

For $x_N\leqslant x<x_{N+1}$, one has
\[
 0\leqslant B(x)-B(x_N)\leqslant mL,\qquad 0\leqslant\tau x-NL<L.
\]
Therefore, $\left| \frac{B(x)}{\tau x} - \frac{B(x_N)}{NL} \right| = O(N^{-1})$ and replacing $x_N$ by $x$ does not change the liminf. This proves \eqref{eq:power-pressure-integral}.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lem-20: body_md -->
\label{lem:power-rank-identity}
Suppose that $\tau\notin\mathcal E_{m,n}$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-rank-identity}
 \frac{B(x)}{\tau}
 =\frac{\displaystyle\int_0^x r_{A,\tau}(\mathrm e^u)\,du
             -\log\mathcal N_A(\mathrm e^{-\tau x},\mathrm e^x)}{1+\tau}
   +o(x).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lem-20 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Outside of the exceptional set $E = \{u:\min_i|g_i(u)|\leqslant H_d\}$,
take $r'$ and $\Gamma_{r'}$ from \eqref{eq:power-small-lattice}
at $(u,\tau u)$.
By definition of $r_{A,\tau}$ and \eqref{eq:power-normalized-minima},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r'_through_r}
r'(u)=\#\{i:g_i(u)<0\}=r_{A,\tau}(\mathrm e^u),
\end{equation}
and
Lemma~\ref{lem:power-maximizer} gives
\[
F(u,\tau u)=-\log|a_{u,\tau u}\omega_{\Gamma_{r'}}|.
\]
The lattice $\Gamma_{r'}$ is locally fixed by
Lemma~\ref{lem:power-gaps}.
Its affine branch changes in \eqref{eq:power-exterior-profile} within a connected component of $[0, x] \setminus E$ occur at finite set of fixed values of $u+y$, so the line $y=\tau u$ meets them only at isolated points.
Away from these points, \eqref{eq:power-exterior-profile} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:equal-deriv}
\frac d{du}F(u,\tau u)
=r'(u)+(1+\tau)\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}(u,\tau u).
\end{equation}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:power-threshold}, $|E\cap[0,x]|=o(x)$.

By \eqref{eq:r'_through_r} and
\eqref{eq:power-profile-comparison},
\[
0\leqslant r_{A,\tau}(\mathrm e^u)\leqslant d,
\qquad
0\leqslant-\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}(u,\tau u)\leqslant m,
\]
with the assumed zero extension of the partial derivative.
Moreover, Lemma~\ref{lem:power-profile} and
\eqref{eq:power-exterior-profile} show that $u\mapsto F(u,\tau u)$
is locally absolutely continuous, with
\[
\left|\frac d{du}F(u,\tau u)\right|\leqslant n+m\tau
\quad\text{almost everywhere}.
\]
Thus the error produced by changing the integration domain from $[0, x]$ to $[0, x] \setminus E$ is $O_{m,n,\tau}(|E\cap[0,x]|) = o(x)$.
Integrating \eqref{eq:equal-deriv} and using \eqref{eq:r'_through_r} gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
F(x,\tau x)-F(0,0)
&=\int_0^x\frac d{du}F(u,\tau u)\,du\\
&=\int_0^x r_{A,\tau}(\mathrm e^u)\,du
 -\frac{1+\tau}{\tau}B(x)+o(x),
\end{aligned}
\]
where the last equality uses the definition of $B$ and
Lemma~\ref{lem:power-threshold}.
Using \eqref{eq:power-profile-comparison} and absorbing the constant
$F(0,0)$ into $o(x)$, we obtain \eqref{eq:power-rank-identity}.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
First let $\psi=\psi_\tau$. The error in
\eqref{eq:power-pressure-integral} is uniform in $c$, so
Theorem~\ref{thm:main} proves $c$-invariance and identifies the
common dimension with the liminf there. Formula
\eqref{eq:power-rank-identity}, with $T=\mathrm e^x$, gives \eqref{eq:power-rank-dimension}.

We next prove that this dimension is continuous in $\tau$ away
from $\mathcal E_{m,n}$. Give the normalized minima an additional
superscript indicating the exponent. Since \(a_{x,\sigma x} =\operatorname{diag}\bigl(\mathrm e^{(\sigma-\tau)x}I_m,I_n\bigr) a_{x,\tau x}\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:minima-Lips}
 |g_i^\sigma(x)-g_i^\tau(x)|\leqslant|\sigma-\tau|x.
\end{equation}
Moreover, \eqref{eq:2.1} and
\eqref{eq:power-normalized-minima} give
\[
\log\mathcal N_A(\mathrm e^{-\tau x},\mathrm e^x)
=\sum_{i=1}^d\max\{-g_i^\tau(x),0\},
\]
thus by \eqref{eq:minima-Lips}
 $$
 |\log\mathcal N_A(\mathrm e^{-\sigma x},\mathrm e^x)
       -\log\mathcal N_A(\mathrm e^{-\tau x},\mathrm e^x)|
 \leqslant d|\sigma-\tau|x.
$$

The quantities $r'(u)$ for $\sigma$ and $\tau$, and therefore (see \eqref{eq:r'_through_r}) the quantities $r_{A,\sigma}(\mathrm e^u)$ and $r_{A,\tau}(\mathrm e^u)$, can differ only where
$\min_i|g_i^\tau(u)|\leqslant|\sigma-\tau|x$, for $0\leqslant u\leqslant x$.
Since both lie in $[0,d]$, Lemma~\ref{lem:power-threshold}
 gives
\[
\lim_{\sigma\to\tau}\limsup_{x\to\infty}
\frac1x\left|
\int_0^x
\bigl(r_{A,\sigma}(\mathrm e^u)-r_{A,\tau}(\mathrm e^u)\bigr)\,du
\right|=0.
\]
Therefore, continuity follows directly from \eqref{eq:power-rank-dimension}.

Finally, suppose that $-\log\psi(T)/\log T\to\tau$. For every
$c>0$ and arbitrarily small $\varepsilon>0$, eventually
\[
 T^{-(\tau+\varepsilon)}\leqslant c\psi(T)
       \leqslant T^{-(\tau-\varepsilon)}.
\]
The corresponding inclusions of approximation sets, followed by
the continuity just proved as $\varepsilon\searrow0$, prove the "Moreover" assertion.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- lem-21: body_md -->
\label{lem:no-drop-powers}
For every $A\in\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}$ and every $\tau>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:no-drop-powers}
 \dim_H\left(\bigcap_{0<\sigma<\tau}D_A(\psi_\sigma)\right)
 =\inf_{0<\sigma<\tau}\dim_HD_A(\psi_\sigma).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lem-21 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Put $D:=\inf_{0<\sigma<\tau}\dim_HD_A(\psi_\sigma)$.
The left-hand side does not exceed the right-hand side by inclusion, so we only need to prove the reverse bound when $D>0$.

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{Step 1: choose transition averages with a common lower bound.}
Fix $L>0$ and $0<\sigma_1<\tau$, choose a sequence $\sigma_p\nearrow\tau$,
and put $T_j=\mathrm e^{jL}$ and
\[
 a_j^{(p)}:=\frac1L\log
 \frac{\mathcal N_A(T_j^{-\sigma_p},T_{j+1})}
      {\mathcal N_A(T_{j+1}^{-\sigma_p},T_{j+1})},
 \qquad j\geqslant0.
\]
By Lemma~\ref{lem:vertical},
$0\leqslant a_j^{(p)}\leqslant m\tau+L^{-1}\log K_d$.
Proposition~\ref{prop:upper} gives \eqref{eq:4.23} with
$d_0=\sigma_1D-L^{-1}\log C_d$, after changing each power on a
bounded initial interval to meet the smallness assumption in
Section~\ref{sec:CD}. Lemma~\ref{lem:diagonal} therefore gives
a nondecreasing sequence $p_j\to\infty$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:exponent-diagonal-average}
 \liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac1N
       \sum_{j=0}^{N-1}a_j^{(p_j)}
 \geqslant \sigma_1D-\frac{\log C_d}{L}.
\end{equation}

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{Step 2: construct a Cantor set with the required dimension bound.}
Put $R_j:=T_j^{-\sigma_{p_j}}$ and choose a continuous strictly
decreasing $\varphi$ with $\varphi(T_j)=R_j$ for all sufficiently
large $j$ and $\varphi(1)<1/12$.
We use Lemmata~\ref{lem:lower-transition} and~\ref{lem:tree}
with $\varphi$ in place of $\psi$ and $k$ sufficiently large,
retaining their notation $\mathbf G_\infty^{(k)}$ and $b_j^{(k)}$. Since $p_j$ is nondecreasing,
$R_j/R_{j+1}\geqslant\mathrm e^{\sigma_1L}$.
Put $\eta_L:=\min\{\mathrm e^L,\mathrm e^{\sigma_1L}\}>1$; then \eqref{eq:2.7} holds for large enough $j$. Put also $c_*(L):=c_d\left(\frac{\eta_L-1}{\eta_L}\right)^d$. Note that Lemma \ref{lem:lower-transition} does not require \eqref{eq:2.5} to hold, so we can use it in the estimate below. One  has
$$
\begin{gathered}
 b_j^{(k)}
 \underset{\eqref{eq:3.25}}{\geqslant}
 c_*(L)
 \frac{\mathcal N_A(R_j,T_{j+1})}
      {\mathcal N_A(R_{j+1},T_{j+1})}
\underset{\substack{
      \eqref{eq:4.2}, \\
      R_{j+1}\leqslant T_{j+1}^{-\sigma_{p_j}}
   }}{\geqslant}
 c_*(L)
 \frac{\mathcal N_A(T_{j}^{-\sigma_{p_j}},T_{j+1})}
      {\mathcal N_A(T_{j+1}^{-\sigma_{p_j}},T_{j+1})}.
\end{gathered}
$$
Taking logarithms and retaining the definition of $a_j^{(p)}$ gives
\[
 \log b_j^{(k)}
 \geqslant La_j^{(p_j)}+\log c_*(L).
\]

















Moreover,
\[ \begin{gathered}
\frac{- \log \varphi(T_{j+1})}{- \log \varphi(T_{j})} = \frac{-\log R_{j+1}}{-\log R_j}
 =\frac{(j+1)\sigma_{p_{j+1}}}{j\sigma_{p_j}}
 \longrightarrow1, 
\end{gathered} \]
so \eqref{eq:tree-log-ratio} holds. Note also that $$-\log \varphi(T_N) =  -\log R_N=NL\sigma_{p_N} \leqslant NL \tau.$$ Lemma~\ref{lem:tree} and \eqref{eq:exponent-diagonal-average}
therefore give
\[
 \dim_H\mathbf G_\infty^{(k)}
 \geqslant \frac{\sigma_1}{\tau}D
 -\frac{\log C_d-\log c_*(L)}{\tau L}.
\]

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{Step 3: verify inclusion in every set in the intersection.}
Fix $0<\sigma<\tau$. Since $j\sigma_{p_j}/(j+1)\to\tau$,
one has $R_j\leqslant T_{j+1}^{-\sigma}$ for all sufficiently large $j$.
For $\mathbf b\in\mathbf G_\infty^{(k)}$ and $T_j\leqslant T<T_{j+1}$,
its level-$j$ ball provides $\mathbf q_j\in\mathbb Z^n$ satisfying
\[
 |\mathbf q_j|\leqslant T_j\leqslant T,
 \qquad
 |A\mathbf q_j-\mathbf b|_{\mathbb Z^m}\leqslant R_j\leqslant T_{j+1}^{-\sigma}\leqslant T^{-\sigma}.
\]
Thus $\mathbf G_\infty^{(k)}\subseteq
\bigcap_{0<\sigma<\tau}D_A(\psi_\sigma)$.
Letting $L\to\infty$, so that $c_*(L)\to c_d>0$, and then
$\sigma_1\nearrow\tau$ proves \eqref{eq:no-drop-powers}.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
By \eqref{eq:power-pressure-ordering}, applied with
$\psi(T)=T^{-1}$, the left limits of $P_-$ and $P_+$ at every $\tau>0$
coincide, as do their right limits. The same limits are obtained
from $\dim_HD_A(\psi_\sigma)$, which lies between them.
Put
\[
 G_\tau:=\{\mathbf b:\widehat\omega(A,\mathbf b)\geqslant\tau\},
 \qquad
 H_\tau:=\{\mathbf b:\widehat\omega(A,\mathbf b)>\tau\}.
\]
The identities
\[
 G_\tau=\bigcap_{0<\sigma<\tau}D_A(\psi_\sigma),
 \qquad
 H_\tau=\bigcup_{\sigma>\tau}D_A(\psi_\sigma)
\]
hold, and both intersections and unions may be restricted to
rational $\sigma$. Lemma~\ref{lem:no-drop-powers} and countable stability, respectively,
give
\[
 \dim_H G_\tau
 =\inf_{0<\sigma<\tau}\dim_HD_A(\psi_\sigma),
 \qquad
 \dim_H H_\tau
 =\sup_{\sigma>\tau}\dim_HD_A(\psi_\sigma).
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:exponent-superlevel-dimension} and \eqref{eq:exponent-strict-superlevel-dimension}.

Suppose $E_\tau:=G_\tau\setminus H_\tau$ is nonempty and choose
$\mathbf b_0\in E_\tau$. One can directly check that $\mathbf b_0+H_\tau\subseteq E_\tau$, thus
$\dim_H H_\tau\leqslant\dim_H E_\tau$. Since
$G_\tau=E_\tau\cup H_\tau$, countable stability proves
$\dim_H E_\tau=\dim_H G_\tau$, as claimed.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
By Theorem~\ref{thm:near-powers}, the common dimension for powers
is continuous at every noncritical exponent.
Thus both one-sided limits in
Proposition~\ref{prop:uniform-exponent-levels} equal
$\dim_HD_A(\psi_\tau)$, completing the proof.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- lem-22: body_md -->
\label{lem:1.9}
Let $\mathcal E\subseteq H_A$ be a $\Gamma_A$-invariant Borel set, and let $f$ be a dimension function. Then $\mathcal E$ has either zero or locally full $\mathcal H^f$-measure in $H_A$.
<!-- end lem-22 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Put $h:=\dim H_A$. If $h=0$, then $H_A$ is finite (a zero-dimensional manifold is discrete and a compact discrete set is finite). Thus $\mathcal H^f(H_A)=0$. We may therefore assume that $h>0$ and $\mathcal H^f(\mathcal E)>0$.

If $\mathcal H^f(\mathcal E)=\infty$, the translates by $\Gamma_A$ of any nonempty relatively open ball in
$H_A$ cover $H_A$, and compactness gives a finite subcover. Since
$\mathcal E$ is $\Gamma_A$-invariant, it follows that
\[
\mathcal H^f(\mathcal E\cap B)=\infty = \mathcal H^f(B)
\]
for every such ball $B$.

Suppose now that $0<\mathcal H^f(\mathcal E)<\infty$; it remains to prove that $\mathcal E$ has locally full $\mathcal H^f$-measure.
Consider the measure
\begin{equation}\label{eq:measure_induced_E}
 F\longmapsto \mathcal H^f(F\cap\mathcal E);
\end{equation}
easy to see that it is well-defined finite Borel measure on $H_A$. This measure is invariant under the dense subgroup $\Gamma_A$ of $H_A$ and thus (see \cite[Lemma 1.1.3]{KSS02}) under $H_A$ itself. Therefore, it is a constant multiple of Haar measure on $H_A$. Since it is concentrated on $\mathcal E$, $\mathcal E$ is Haar-conull.
